In a generic body whose error type is a type parameter, the conversion is chosen per instance (section 5), so this EN0820 depends on the instance, with its primary label at the instantiating call (above), and an instance whose `E` is `Failure` moves the error and does not count. With no `@globalAllocator` every `Failure` therefore has `cap == 0`, since a template or a copied `&string` in `Err`, a conversion that builds a text and an owned `string` that `Err` could move in are already EN0820, so `Failure`'s drop glue, which frees only when `cap > 0`, is trivial there and is not a call into `alloc`.

**EN0824** comes from separate BFS walks, one from each `@interrupt` function, one from the `@panicHandler` and one from the methods of the `@globalAllocator`. A walk fails on reaching the `__rt_alloc` or `__rt_dealloc` lang items (every allocating or freeing library path goes through them, drop glue included) or, from the first two roots, any method of the `@globalAllocator` static. Address-taken functions count as reached, so a `fn` value cannot hide allocation. There is no run-time lock, so the 2 KB allocator budget holds.

**Phase order** (SPEC 13.1), enforced by the driver; each phase reports all its errors and stops the compile at its end:

1. Lexical and syntax.
2. Resolve and types. Typing never reads a contract: member lookup, inference and interface shape ignore permissions (N1), so `implements`, bounds and EN0612 are checked here by shape alone, except that a bound naming a class is met nominally, by that class or a subclass (section 6.3). Once a module's bodies are typed, each type parameter bound to a view is checked against its function's `views_ok`, each one bound to `string` against its `strings_ok`, and each member recorded as satisfying an interface member, by any satisfaction, against that member's `strings_ok` (section 4). Every type meets a `Display` bound, a type parameter with no bound written included, and a class or enum does so through a written `fmt`, its own or inherited, or a synthesised one.
3. Moves and borrows. Contracts come first, per SCC of the call graph with a node per borrowing class, and on demand (section 6.7), and a module read from its interface file has its stored MIR verified (section 2.5), its contracts and flags recomputed from it, and all of this phase run again over its stored declarations and bodies, every comparison below included, EN0316 on a failure or a mismatch, or, for a module with no source rechecked after an import's contracts changed, the ordinary code at the stored span (section 6.8); then moves, borrows, every permission comparison (EN0615 for an interface, an override, a bound or a `fn` coercion, and EN0714 for result sources outside the declared set), EN0418 for `implements Copy` on a class whose flag is set (section 6.7), and the narrowing check of SPEC 4.8 (N2). Phase 2 errors stop the compile, so no contract comes from a badly typed body.
4. Level and layout: `asm` templates, EN09xx, `@bits`, EN0804 on every MIR `Ref` whose place crosses a `@packed` field with no later `Deref` and whose referent type has alignment above 1 (SPEC 7.6), EN0435.
5. Reachability: root collection (EN0310, and EN0809, which also refuses top-level statements on a freestanding target), mono-time EN0414 and EN0435, EN0812, EN0820, EN0823, EN0824, EN1201-EN1203, EN1205. `check` and compile tests run this phase only if top-level statements, a `main` or an `@entry` exist; `build`, `run` and `dev` always do.

User-module bodies are always checked. Library bodies are typed on first demand, when a contract needs them in phase 3 or mono reaches them, so unreached library bodies stay untyped; a meta-test checks the whole library per target. A diagnostic in library code is an internal error, exit 3.

### 2.7 TargetSpec

```rust
/// A target-sized quantity. Compiler-side sizes and addresses are u64; this checks them against the spec.
pub struct TargetUsize(pub u64);
impl TargetUsize { pub fn checked(v: u64, spec: &TargetSpec) -> Option<TargetUsize>; }

pub enum Arch { I386, X86_64, Mir }
pub enum CodeMode { M32, M64 }
pub struct DataLayout { pub ptr_bytes: u32, pub i64_align: u32, pub f64_align: u32, pub frame_align: u32 }
pub enum FloatStrategy { Soft, Sse2, X87 /* i386-* only; code generation from M8 */, Host /* VM only */ }
pub enum OsLayer { LinuxX64, LinuxInt80, Win64Imports, None, Uefi, Vm }
pub enum OutputFormat { Elf64, Elf32, Pe { subsystem: u16 }, Flat, BiosImage(ImageKind), None }
pub enum ImageKind { Floppy144, Hdd }
pub enum Addressing { RipRelative, Absolute32 }
pub struct CallConv {
    pub int_args: &'static [Reg], pub float_args: &'static [Xmm], pub int_ret: &'static [Reg],
    pub callee_saved: &'static [Reg], pub scratch: &'static [Reg],
    pub alloc_callee: &'static [Reg], pub alloc_caller: &'static [Reg],
    pub xmm_alloc: &'static [Xmm], pub xmm_scratch: &'static [Xmm],
    pub stack_args_callee_pops: bool,
}
pub struct ForeignAbi { pub kind: ForeignKind /* None | SysV | Win64 */, pub shadow_bytes: u32,
    pub extra_saved: &'static [Reg], pub extra_saved_xmm: &'static [Xmm] }
pub struct ImageParams { pub base: u64, pub page_size: u32, pub section_align: u32, pub file_align: u32 }
pub struct StackPolicy { pub size: u64, pub probe_interval: Option<u32>, pub limit_check: bool, pub limit_reserve: u32 }
pub enum PanicPolicy { Exit(i32), UserHandler, VmPause }
pub enum TraceLevel { Full, Fn, None }

pub struct TargetSpec {
    pub name: &'static str, pub arch: Arch, pub code_mode: CodeMode, pub layout: DataLayout,
    pub cpu: CpuLevel, pub allowed: FeatureSet, pub floor: FeatureSet, pub float: FloatStrategy,
    pub cc: CallConv, pub foreign: ForeignAbi, pub os: OsLayer, pub alloc_default: bool,
    pub format: OutputFormat, pub addressing: Addressing, pub image: ImageParams,
    pub stack: StackPolicy, pub panic: PanicPolicy, pub trace: TraceLevel,
}
```

There is no red-zone field because no target ever uses one, and no atomics field because availability follows from `allowed`.

| Target | P | Default `--cpu` | Default float | Format | Base | OS layer |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `x86_64-linux` | 8 | `x86-64` | `sse2` | ELF64 ET_EXEC | `0x400000` | raw `syscall` |
| `x86_64-windows` | 8 | `x86-64` | `sse2` | PE32+ CUI | `0x140000000` | `kernel32.dll` imports |
| `i386-linux` | 4 | `i386` | `soft` | ELF32 ET_EXEC | `0x08048000` | `int 0x80` |
| `i386-none` | 4 | `i386` | `soft` | flat or BIOS image | `0x10000` | none |
| `x86_64-none` | 8 | `x86-64` | `sse2` | flat, BIOS image, or PE32+ EFI | `0x100000` | none or UEFI |
| `vm64` | 8 | n/a | host or `--float=soft` | none | n/a | `os/vm` |
| `vm32` | 4 | n/a | host or `--float=soft` | none | n/a | `os/vm` |

On `i386-*` the default float follows the level: `soft` at `i386` and `i486`, `x87` at `i586` and `i686`, and `sse2` at `sse2`. `--float=soft` is legal on every `i386-*` and VM row and is EN0911 on native `x86_64-*`, which has only `sse2`; `--float=sse2` below level `sse2` is EN0911; `--float=x87` is legal at every level of `i386-*` and is EN0911 on native `x86_64-*`; `i386-windows` is EN1031. `--float=sse2` and `--float=x87` on a VM row are EN0911 too. Any `--cpu` on a VM row, and `x86-64` on `i386-*`, are EN0912. An unknown `--float`, `--format`, `--image` or `--trace` value is EN1304.

**Rule: no compiler code assumes a 64-bit `usize`.** Target sizes are `u64` with `TargetUsize` checks, literals are range-checked against the resolved target (EN0411), and the corpus runs on `vm32` before any i386 backend exists, so a 64-bit assumption in the library or in mono fails at M4.

## 4. Front-end algorithms

**Lexer and parser.** Hand-written, recursive descent with Pratt for SPEC 3.5. Recovery: a missing `;` assumes it at the next line start and folds repeats into the first site's diagnostic, with a count and every insertion in its fix-it; a bad item skips to the next item keyword at brace depth 0; a bad statement skips to `;` or `}`. EN0102 follows the indentation comparison in SPEC 2 and recovers as if its fix-it's `}` were present, so G02 is derived and not guessed. TS habits are recognised at the token (`===`, `var`, `any`, `number`, `export`) and given EN02xx with recovery as the Enrang form. Expression-context `<` follows SPEC 3.4: it speculates by checkpointing the token index, only with no space before it. `split_token` handles `>>`, `>>>`, `>=`, `>>=`, `**` and `&&` on the parser's request. `parse_array_elem` is the only consumer of `...`: anywhere else the parser reports EN1013 at the token, skips it and goes on, and an `=` after an `ArrayLit` holding a spread reports EN1013 at `dots`, with no EN1042 and no EN0114 (SPEC 3.4). A `= default` in a destructuring pattern is syntactic, EN1010 in phase 1. Whether a pattern destructures an array, `T[]` or `T[N]` at any depth, needs the source's type, so sema reports it in phase 2 as EN1042 and the parser never does: `const { pair: [a, b] } = h;` is a tuple copy or EN1042 by the type of `pair`. The parser keeps what the formatter prints back, each number literal's spelling and each pair of parentheses around an expression as a paren node (section 2.3), and keeps a program's top-level statements and local functions in `File.stmts`; a statement after a class or other declaration is EN0115. Tests live only in `.test.en` parts, and a test item in any other file is EN1112 in either mode. `ParseMode::Build` loads no `.test.en` part, so a module builds byte-identical with or without its tests, and its test items change nothing the module's own code depends on: they see no class's `private` members (EN0308, **Class members** below), so they set no borrowing-class flag, and the module's contracts, layouts and API hash are the same in both modes (section 6.7). `readonly` before a `&` parses as `ReadonlyRef` in a field's type, at the field or at a reference part of its tuple, as it parses before a return type; nowhere else. `check` uses `ParseMode::Test` and loads `.test.en` parts, so errors in test items are never skipped.

**Modules.** The loader walks `import`s from the entry, mapping `core`, `alloc`, `os` to `enrang-rtlib` (`os` by `TargetSpec.os`). A cycle is EN0304, re-exports (`public import`) included. Modules are then checked in topological order of their imports, with ties broken by module path, so each module's imports have their contracts before it is checked and no order depends on how the file system lists a directory (section 6.8). Importing a file with top-level statements is EN0312, and importing a `private` item is EN0311. `foo.test.en` is an extra part of module `foo` in `ParseMode::Test`: a unit above the module, whose own imports are not counted for EN0304 (SPEC 4.12). It sees the module's `private` items, its functions, classes, enums, aliases and constants, but never a class's `private` members (**Class members**, below). The prelude is injected per target layer, and `alloc`'s signatures are in it on every target (SPEC 8.1).

**Resolution** has two passes over items, then body-local scopes. A name resolves to `Res::Local`, `Res::Def`, `Res::PrimType` (for `i32.MAX`) or `Res::Namespace(ModuleId)`, following the binding rule of SPEC 4.12, which gives user folders and `import * as` their namespaces. A namespace is legal only left of `.`. A program's top-level variables resolve in its statements and in the local functions declared below them, and never in a class body (EN0313). That declared-above check reads only the names written in a function's body. A local function's hidden arguments (N13) are the top-level variables it uses plus those of every local function it calls, a least fixed point over the local call graph, so `relay()`, which names no variable but calls `grow()`, lends `q` as `grow` does. A local function whose set is not empty may be called only among the statements and from other local functions; a call anywhere else, in a class body or an item function, is EN0313, because nothing there can evaluate its hidden arguments, and a `.test.en` part does not see local functions at all (SPEC 12.1). For the same reason a decorator that makes the compiler, a runtime or foreign code its caller (`@panicHandler`, `@devMigrate`, `@entry`, `@interrupt`, `@naked`, `@abi`) is EN0806 on such a function, while `@mustUse`, `@section`, `@requires` and `@was` stay allowed. Whether a local function may become an `fn` value (EN0803) or a closure argument (C12) is decided on the same set. M1's parser accepts `extend` declarations, `enrang-sema` collects them, and `Tcx::member` reads those in the import closure of the module doing the lookup. "Did you mean" draws on the scope at the error site and on members for EN0303, using Damerau-Levenshtein with threshold `max(1, len/3)`.

**Types.** Bidirectional per body, union-find inference variables with kinds `Any`, `Int`, `Float`. Variables unify across the whole function. An `Int` variable compared with an operand of a known integer type takes that type, while two integers of different known types compare by value (SPEC 4.6, 4.7). Defaults apply at the end: `int` (`i32`) for integers, `f64` for floats. `check_expr(e, expected)` records `Adjust`s at each coercion site from the SPEC 4.5 table and nowhere else; nothing nests. At one site the adjusts form at most one chain, at the top only: one step from a value to a reference or one deref, upcast or interface conversion (C13, C04, C21, C22), then C18, then one wrap (C06 or C07), as an arrow at a nullable closure parameter takes C12 then C06 (below), and `return d;` with `d: &Dog` at `&Speaker | null` records `RefToInterface` then `WrapNullRef`. So `read(0, &sector)` with `buf?: &Slice<u8>` records `FixedToSlice` then `WrapNullRef`, and `fill(&b, 7)` with `b: Box<u8[]>` records `BoxDeref` then `ArrayToSlice`. Overload matching (SPEC 4.14) uses these chains, never a numeric conversion, so `sum(&f)` with `f: i64[3]` picks the `&Slice<i64>` overload over the `&Slice<f64>` one (`TESTING.md` `SL-06-chain`), and a literal follows its default. Among the overloads a call matches, it ranks in three steps, in order: (1) a non-generic overload beats a generic one; (2) between two of the same kind, one that needs no chain beats one that needs a chain; (3) between two generic overloads still tied, the more specific one wins, where `F` is more specific than `G` when every parameter type of `F` can be passed to `G`'s, `G`'s type parameters bound by `F`'s types, and not the reverse, as C++'s partial ordering and Swift rank them, so `&Slice<T>` is more specific than `&T`. No step reads `views_ok`, which is known only once every body is typed, from the callees those bodies resolve to (**Position checks**, below). So `describe(&rex)` on a `Dog` runs `describe(a: &Animal)`, which needs C21, and not `describe<T extends Animal>(a: &T)`, which needs no chain; `total(&arr)` on an `i64[3]` runs `total(xs: &Slice<i64>)` and not `total<T>(x: &T)`; and with `show<T>(x: &T)` and `show<T>(x: &Slice<T>)`, `show(sector.slice(0, 2))` runs the second, the more specific, and `show(&sector)` the first, the only one that needs no chain (`VI-07-overloads`). A call that no overload matches is EN0451, which lists the candidates, and so is a call that two or more overloads still match equally after the three steps, which lists those overloads: `f<T>(a: &T, b: &Slice<i64>)` and `f<T>(a: &Slice<i64>, b: &T)`, neither more specific than the other, make `f(v, v)` EN0451 for a `v: &Slice<i64>`. At a `&Slice<T>`, C18 records `FixedToSlice` for a `&T[N]` and `ArrayToSlice` for a whole `&T[]`. A `&T[N]` or a `&Slice<T>` where a `&T[]` is expected has no coercion, because only a whole growable array can grow: it is EN0401, `a fixed array can't grow` or `a part of an array can't grow` (SPEC 4.3). Typing cannot tell whether the callee pushes (N1), so the help for a fixed array gives both ways, take `xs: &Slice<u8>` if `addOne` only reads or sets elements, or make `sector` a `u8[]` to let it grow; for a part, `help: pass the whole array: addOne(&list)` appears only when the argument is a `slice` call on a whole-array place, and otherwise the help names both parameter types. An optional parameter `x?: T` has type `T | null` and default `null`, or `T` itself when `T` as written is already nullable, so `width?: i64 | null` is `width: i64 | null = null` and `width ?? 8` is `i64`. The collapse is decided on the type as written where `?` is written, for an optional field too, and a type parameter is never already nullable there, so with `type Opt<T> = { v?: T }`, `Opt<i64 | null>` is `{ v: (i64 | null) | null }`, which a generic body storing a `T` into `v` relies on (SPEC 4.10), and not the type a written `{ v?: i64 | null }` is. Generic calls follow the steps of SPEC 4.7: the return type against the expected type, as a hint, then non-arrow arguments left to right (with `?T` against `&U` pending to the end of the call), then arrows. If a non-arrow argument fits its parameter neither as it is nor by a coercion, the hint is dropped, the call is typed again from its arguments alone, and its result is coerced at its own site (C21, C22); when both ways fail, the error reported is the one with the hint. So in `const pets: Box<Animal>[] = [new Box(new Dog("Fido"))];` the element is `Box<Dog>` before C21 makes it `Box<Animal>`, `Ok(x)` with `x: i64` at `Result<i64 | null>` keeps `T := i64 | null` and wraps `x` by C06, and `new Box(new Echo(&text))` at `Box<Speaker>` is EN0701 both ways. A tuple pattern, in a declaration or an assignment, gives its right side an expected tuple type whose parts are the targets' types, or fresh variables in a declaration, so a bracket literal there is a tuple: `[a, b] = [b, a];` is a swap, never an array destructuring. An object pattern gives no expected type. At a C19 site in a function returning `Result<T, E>`, an operand whose type comes from context (a bracket or object literal, `[]`, `null`, a number literal, or a string literal at a literal-union type) is checked against `T` and then wrapped, so `return [lo, hi];` and `return null;` work; any other operand is typed as before and wrapped when its type is `T`. EN0455 fires when that operand would also be an `Err` at `E` without a conversion: at `E` = `Failure`, a fixed text or a template, or any other `string` or `&string` operand that `T` also accepts, so `return msg;` with `msg: string` in a `Result<string>` function is EN0455, `` `msg` could be a success or an error here ``, with `` help: write `Ok(msg)` or `Err(msg)` ``, and for an operand that no move may leave, as `return this.name;` in a `Result<string>` method, with the forms that compile, `` help: write `Ok(this.name.clone())` or `Err(&this.name)` `` (`TESTING.md` `S2-33-err`); a fixed text at a literal-union `E`; or a value whose own type is `E`. An error value of another type is not, although `Err` would convert it, so `return Token.End;` in a `Result<Token>` function returns `Ok`. `Err(x)` records its `ErrForm`: `Value` when `x` is an `E`; `Text` for a fixed text or a template at `Failure`, or a fixed text at a literal-union `E`; at `Failure`, `TextMove` for any other `string` operand, which moves in with no copy, and `TextCopy` for an operand of type `&string`, which is copied; and `IntoFailure` for an error value of another type at `E` = `Failure`; anything else is EN0401, since at a named `E` `Err` needs that `E`. A `string` place that no move may leave, one reached through a reference or an index, is never copied in secret: it is `TextMove` like any other `string` operand, so phase 3 reports EN0603, EN0604 or EN0605 for `Err(this.name)` or `Err(names[0])`, as for `const n = this.name;`, with `` help: copy the text: `Err(&this.name)` `` and the help that names `this.name.clone()`, and a captured `string`, as `Err(last)` in an arrow, is EN0617 with the same help. A member access on an `Any` variable is immediately EN0410. On an `Int` or `Float` variable it looks up `__Int` or `__Float` with `Self` set to that variable. A `where` clause is checked at the call after inference. Float literals stay as text until inference settles their width.

**Array spread** follows SPEC 4.13. `check_array` checks each spread operand with no expected type, records `AutoBorrow` on a place and `Reborrow` on a reference, then `ArrayToSlice` or `FixedToSlice` on an array, for the `extendCloned` call it lowers to (section 5), and unifies the element type. All of these only read, so phase 3 never makes them exclusive. The `is_clone` check waits for the end of inference, and its failure is EN0438. A literal with a spread becomes `ArrayBuild`, and the const interpreter never sees one, because `...` in a `ConstExpr` is EN0412 at that token during typing.

**Position checks (R1, R2).** Every element, payload and generic argument type must satisfy `is_value_type`, except that `Box<I>` takes an interface `I` (section 2.4). A field may hold a reference only if it is `private` (EN0701). A field whose type is a borrowing tuple, or contains one outside any borrowing-class field, as `[&i64[], i32]` and `[&i64[], i32] | null` do, counts as a reference field: it must be `private` too, and it sets its class's flag unless every reference part of it, at every depth, a nullable payload included, is declared `readonly` (section 6.7). A reference field, or a field whose type is a borrowing class, makes its class borrowing, and a tuple with a reference part is borrowing by the same rule (SPEC 5.1); being ref-carrying, such a type then fails `is_value_type` wherever it appears. EN0701 covers every generic argument and comes first, so `Result<i64, &AgeError>` is EN0701 alone, with no EN0454. The class that C22 turns into `I`, `&I` or `Box<I>`, and a value that C20 passes to a by-value object parameter, must satisfy `is_value_type` as well, because an interface or shape copy is instantiated like a generic, whose `T` ranges over `V`: `wrap(new View(&tmp))` with `wrap(s: Shower)` is EN0701, `` a `Shower` can't hold a `View`, because `View` holds a reference ``. A by-value parameter of an object type, a fields-only interface included, is an implicit type parameter bounded by that shape, and two such parameters are two type parameters, so with `rename(let a: { name: string }, b: { name: string })`, `a = b` is EN0401, `` `b` may hold another class than `a` `` (`TESTING.md` `VI-03-swap`). In the body it may go to another per-shape parameter, behind a reference or to a generic `T`, so `dispose(x)` drops the real class; moving it whole into storage that its declared type fixes (a result, an annotated local, a field, an array element, a `Box`) is EN0440, `` `x` may have more fields than `{ name: string }` ``, with the help `` build the smaller object: `{ name: x.name }` ``. `dispose` and `mem.forget` are compiler forms (`Callee::Form`, SPEC 6), not generic functions, so they take any `S` value by move: a borrowing value ends at the call, where `dispose` runs its destructor and `mem.forget` skips it, and a reference passed to either ends nothing (SPEC 5.7 D5). Every parameter, local and return type must satisfy `is_slot_type`. A closure type is written only as a parameter type (EN0705), where `| null` at its top is not nesting, so `handler?: (k: u8) => void` is `((k: u8) => void) | null = null`; a local holding a capturing arrow gets one by inference. `Slice<T>` without `&` in front, as a variable, field, element or result, or as a type argument for a type parameter without `views_ok` (below), is EN0401 (SPEC 4.3). A type parameter that its function's signature and body use only behind `&`, never by value, never in `sizeOf`, `alignOf` or `offsetOf`, and never as an element, a field, a payload, a local, a parameter or a result, nor as a type argument that needs a value type, never behind a raw pointer, and never converted by C22, as `T`, `&T` or `Box<T>` to `I`, `&I` or `Box<I>`, may stand for a view: it has `views_ok`, so `show(sector.slice(0, 2))` with G04's `show<T>(x: &T)` infers `T := Slice<u8>` and compiles, and the instance's `&T` is two words (section 2.6, `TESTING.md` `SL-10-helps`). `views_ok` is a type fact, not a permission, computed once a module's bodies are typed, per SCC of the call graph, as a greatest fixed point: every type parameter of a declaration with a body starts with it and loses it at a by-value use; at a C22 conversion of `T`, `&T` or `Box<T>`, since only a value type becomes an interface value (SPEC 4.4) and the second word of a two-word `&I` is its table, with no room for a view's length; at any `*T`, a cast, a local, a parameter, a field, `ptr.null<T>()` or a pointer method, since a view stands only behind `&`; or where it is a type argument of a parameter that lacks it. A declaration with no body, an interface member, an abstract method, a `declare function` or an `@intrinsic` member, has no body to keep it, so it lacks it, unless the compiler table that gives an intrinsic its contract states it, and a call through one binds no view, whatever the bodies that implement it do: `d.dump(v)` with `Dumper.dump<T>(x: &T)` is EN0401, with `` note: `Dumper.dump` has no body, so its `T` may be used by value `` (`TESTING.md` `VI-05-dumper`). A method with type parameters of its own is never overridable (section 2.6), so no override has a type parameter of its own whose fact could differ from its base declaration's: `public override show<T>(x: &T)` in a `Dog` over `Animal.show<T>(x: &T)` is EN0460 at the override whatever its body does (**Class members**, below, `VI-05-override`). The fact is stored with each declaration, every method's included, in the interface file's hashed section, so the API hash and the used-items hash cover it and an import that loses it rebuilds its importers, or is EN0315 for one with no source, never a recheck (section 6.8); the check on load recomputes it from the stored MIR, as it recomputes contracts, and a stated fact that differs is EN0316. A `T` bounded by a class never has it, since no view extends a class. Binding a view to a `T` without it is EN0401, `` `T` can't stand for the view `Slice<u8>` ``, with a label at the use in the body that loses it, `const m: &Measured = x;` for a C22 conversion in `show<T extends Measured>(x: &T)` and `x as *T` for a cast (`VI-02-measured`, `VI-02-cast`), and `` help: add `show<T>(x: &Slice<T>)` ``. That help adds a generic overload that also matches a view with no chain, and step (3) of the ranking in **Types** (above) prefers it, since `&Slice<T>` is more specific than `&T`, so once it is added `show(sector.slice(0, 2))` runs it and binds no view to the first overload's `T`, while `show(&sector)` still runs the first, the only one that needs no chain (`Q44-show`, `VI-07-overloads`). The same fixed point computes `strings_ok`, whether a type parameter may stand for `string`: a `&string` never becomes an `&I` (**Interface types**, below), and an instance at `T := string` has no whole descriptor for a C22 conversion of its `&T`, which is a pointer and a length when it only reads (section 7.7), so a type parameter loses it at a C22 conversion of a `&T` or `&T | null` to an interface reference, in its body or in an arrow nested in it at any depth, whose MIR body counts as part of the declaration's for this fact and for `views_ok`, and where it is a type argument of a parameter, or of a class, that lacks it. A class's type parameter has `strings_ok` too, computed by the same fixed point over all of the class's bodies, its methods, `static` ones included, its constructors, its destructor and the arrows nested in them, and binding `string` to one without it, as `new Keeper<string> {}` for a `Keeper<T extends Measured>` whose `show(x: &T)` converts `x` to a `&Measured`, is EN0401 (`TESTING.md` `VI-02-class`). An interface member has it, so a call through one binds `string` freely, and a member that satisfies one, which such a call may reach, must keep it. That is a clause of satisfaction (SPEC 4.4), run by the one satisfaction routine that every site using a class as an interface calls, an `implements` entry, a C22 conversion, an argument to a non-null `&I` or by-value `I` parameter and a bound `T extends I` met by a written or inferred type argument: the routine records each (class, interface, member) it finds, own, inherited or `extend`, and once every body's `strings_ok` is known it checks each recorded member's type parameters against the interface member's, EN0401 at the member's conversion with `` note: `Keeper` is used as a `Sizer` here `` at the site and `` note: `Sizer.size` lets `T` stand for `string` ``, once per member and interface; phase 3's fitting runs over the same recorded set (section 6.7). So M2's `check_implementations` (`enrang-sema`, `passes/views.rs`) is called from that routine, never as a loop over own methods times `implements` (`TESTING.md` `VI-02-structural`, `VI-02-inherited`, `VI-02-bound`); any other declaration with no body lacks it, as for `views_ok`. Binding `string` to a `T` without it is EN0401, `` a `&string` can't become a `&Measured`, because it may be a part of a string ``, labelled at the conversion in the body, with `` help: add `show(x: &string)` ``, a non-generic overload that step (1) then prefers: with `VI-02-measured`'s `show`, `show(s.slice(1, 3))` and `show(&s)` are each EN0401. It is stored and rechecked as `views_ok` is. These checks run on resolved types, so aliases cannot hide a reference.

**Interface types (SPEC 4.4).** `&I` may stand wherever a reference may: a parameter, a local, a result, a borrowing class's `private` field (EN0701 as for any reference field), a tuple part and `&I | null`; a by-value `I` outside a parameter stays EN1006, and a borrowing class still never becomes an interface value (EN0701). Nor does a `&string` become an `&I`, at a parameter or anywhere else, whether it names a whole string, a part, a literal or a `&string` parameter, because it may be a part of a string, whose length an `&I`'s two words, the object and its table, leave no room for (section 7.7): with `public interface Measured { length(): isize; }`, `const m: &Measured = s.slice(1, 3);`, `const k = keep(s.slice(1, 3));` with `keep(m: &Measured): &Measured` and `const w: &Measured = &s;` over a whole `s` are each EN0401, `` a `&string` can't become a `&Measured`, because it may be a part of a string ``, with `help: take <T extends Measured>(m: &T)` and `help: or keep a copy: s.slice(1, 3).toString()`. The generic form binds `T := string` and passes the part on as it is, through SPEC 11's statement-temporary descriptor, so `console.log(keepT(s.slice(1, 3)).length());` with `keepT<T extends Measured>(m: &T): &T` prints `2`, while a `Box<string>` holding `hello` still becomes a `Box<Measured>`, whose `length()` prints `5`, and a by-value `string` still reaches a by-value `I` parameter, since each holds a whole descriptor (`TESTING.md` `VI-01-part`). A generic body that itself converts its `&T` to an `&I` has no `strings_ok`, so it takes no `string` (**Position checks**, above, `VI-02-measured`). In a body, each interface-typed parameter, and each two-word `&I` value, taken from a box or read from a local, a result, a field, a tuple part or a nullable, stands for a class of its own, an implicit `X extends I`, and `Self` in a member called on it means that `X`. So with `a: &Eq` and `b: &Eq`, `a.equals(b)` is EN0401 at `b`, `` `b` may hold another class than `a` ``, with `help: use one type for both: same<T extends Eq>(a: &T, b: &T)`, because the copy for a `Dog` and a `Robot` would pass a `Robot` where `Dog.equals` reads a `Dog` (`TESTING.md` `S2-09-self`). The body types each interface-typed parameter, by value or behind `&`, as that class of its own. Behind `&` it still converts to `&I` by C22 at any coercion site, in the two-word form (`RefToInterface`, section 2.4), so `pick` and `let t: &Speaker = a; t = b;` compile (`S2-17-pick`), while by value it converts only as a whole argument to a by-value `I` parameter, which reaches the copy for its own class: with `swapIn(let s: Speaker, o: Speaker)`, `s = o` is EN0401, `` `o` may hold another class than `s` ``, with `help: use one type for both: swapIn<T extends Speaker>(let s: T, o: T)` (`VI-03-swap`). A by-value `I` parameter takes a whole argument whose type is one class, or the class of one interface parameter, since the copy it reaches is chosen at compile time, and C22 never converts a ternary branch, a `match` arm or a `??` operand to a by-value `I`: with `relay(x: Speaker)`, `relay(c ? new Dog {} : new Robot {})` is EN1006 at the ternary, with `` note: this `Speaker` may be a `Dog` or a `Robot`, so its class isn't known `` and `help: call relay in each branch`, and so is `relay(c ? s : o)` over two `Speaker` parameters, while `relay(c ? new Dog {} : new Dog {})` has one class and compiles (`VI-03-branches`). A member that takes or returns `Self` by value, as `clone` returns it, cannot run where the class is unknown, and neither can a member compiled once per type, with type parameters of its own, a by-value interface parameter or an object-type parameter, as `Dumper.dump<T>(x: &T)` is, which no table entry can name (section 2.6), so a `Box<I>` of such an `I` is EN1006, and so is an `&I` of one wherever it needs the two-word form: anywhere but at a non-null `&I` parameter, and also as an argument of a call whose callee is chosen at run time and as a parameter of a function converted to an `fn` value or a closure parameter (section 2.6). A function with a by-value `I` parameter, which has a copy per class and none for an unknown class, is EN1006 where it is converted to an `fn` value or passed as a closure. An interface type by itself is never a value type (section 2.4), so no type parameter stands for one, inferred or written, and no generic body moves or swaps an object whose size it does not know: `mem.swap(&*a, &*b)` over two `Box<Speaker>` is EN1006, `` `Speaker` is an interface, so the size of `*a` isn't known ``, with `help: swap the boxes: mem.swap(&a, &b)`, `mem.replace(&*a, v)` is EN1006 the same way, and `show(&*a)` with G04's `show<T>(x: &T)` is EN1006 with the help `show(&a)`, which prints the contents (`S2-10-swap`).

**Class members (SPEC 4.3).** A `private` member, field or method, is visible only inside its class's body: a subclass, another module and the module's own `.test.en` part each get EN0308 for it, as TypeScript's TS2341 does, while the module's `private` top-level items stay visible to that part (**Modules**, above). A `protected` member is visible in its class and its subclasses, so a test reaches one only from a subclass it declares. A subclass may give an inherited field that it sees a new default, `protected override sound: string = "woof";`: member lookup resolves the name to the base's `FieldIdx`, so it is the same field, not a second one, with the base's type, `readonly` and visibility, and a declaration that differs in any of them replaces nothing and is EN0443, as a method override that matches no overload is. `override` on a field that replaces nothing is EN0443, and a field with the name of a visible inherited one but no `override` is EN0444. A `private` base field is invisible there, so a field of the same name is a new one. The new default follows the rules of a field initialiser, with no `this` (EN0610), and section 5 says when it is stored. `implements Copy` needs every field, inherited ones included, to be Copy, and no destructor in the class or in any base class, so that the class has no drop glue; Copy is never inherited, so only a class that declares `implements Copy` is Copy, and with a `Region` that has a destructor, `public class Snapshot extends Region implements Copy {}` is EN0418 at `Snapshot`, `` `Region`'s destructor runs for every `Snapshot`, so copying one would run it twice ``, with `` help: remove `implements Copy`; to copy a `Snapshot`, write `clone()` `` (`TESTING.md` `VI-04-copy`); phase 3 adds the condition on the class's flag (section 6.7). A field's type may write `readonly` before a reference's `&`, `private src: readonly &Slice<u8>;`, recorded in `FieldDef.readonly_refs` (section 2.4) and enforced in phase 3 (sections 6.1 and 6.7); it combines with the `readonly` modifier, `private readonly src: readonly &Slice<u8>;`, which only keeps the field from being reassigned after construction. A bound may name a class, `T extends Animal`, which that class or any subclass meets, by name, and only that class when it is `final`; in the body `T` has the class's `public` members, its fields read and written as through a `&Animal`, but none of its `protected` ones, since the function is no subclass. A borrowing class as a bound is EN0701, as it can never be a type argument, and a class-bounded `T` is a value type, never a view. Section 6.3 gives a call on such a `T` its contract and dispatch. A method compiled once per type, one with type parameters of its own, a by-value interface parameter or an object-type parameter, may be declared in any class, but no single function could fill a table slot for it, so it is never overridable (section 2.6): it has no slot, and every call to it is a direct call of its declaring class's body, whatever the receiver (section 6.3). Where a subclass can see such a method, an `override` of it, or a subclass method with its signature, is EN0460 at the subclass, `` `show` has its own type parameters, so it can't be overridden ``, with `help: give Dog's method another name`, and so is an `abstract` one, which no subclass could implement, at its declaration; a subclass's method of the same name and another signature is an overload as usual (SPEC 4.14), and over a `private` one it is a new method, as for any `private` method. So with `public show<T>(x: &T): void` in `Animal` and no `show` in `Dog`, `greet(&rex)`, where `greet(p: &Animal)` calls `p.show` with an `i64` and with a string, runs `Animal.show` both times and prints `Animal.show: 42` and `Animal.show: hi` (`TESTING.md` `VI-06-tables`). A `move` method takes the whole object as `this` and disposes of what it leaves with its own class's glue, so its body runs only on an object of exactly its class (section 5, **Exact-class checks**), and phase 2 reports each call that is certain to break that outside generic code and per-class copies: EN0458, `` `move` method not declared in the receiver's class ``, at a call of a `move` method whose receiver's static type is a class `D`, not a type parameter, and whose body is declared in a proper base of `D`, which `D` inherits without overriding it, `this.m()` in a `move` method included, and at every `super.m()` whose target is a `move` method. With `public move build(): string[] { return this.parts; }` in `Builder` and a `HtmlBuilder extends Builder` that does not override it, `b.build()` on a `HtmlBuilder` is EN0458, labelled `` `HtmlBuilder` inherits `build` from `Builder` ``, with `` help: override `build` in `HtmlBuilder` ``, and an override `public override move build(): string[] { return super.build(); }` is EN0458 at `super.build()`, with `` help: move the state out with a helper the base shares, `protected takeParts(): string[] { return mem.replace(&this.parts, []); }` ``. That helper is the documented way to share consuming logic: a `protected` method that is not `move` takes the state out with `mem.replace`, and each class's own `move` method calls it, so `public override move build(): string[] { return this.takeParts(); }` compiles, and after `b.add("a")` on a `HtmlBuilder`, `console.log(b.build());` prints `['a']` (`TESTING.md` `C4-move-inherited`). A `move` call on a `T extends C` receiver is generic code, which instantiation adds no errors to (section 2.6), so it keeps the run-time entry check instead (section 5), and so does a `move` member called on an interface-typed parameter, which a per-class copy for a class that can be extended calls through the method table: with `consume(b: Buildable)` calling `b.build()`, `consume(new HtmlBuilder())` over a `HtmlBuilder` that inherits `build` panics on entry to it (`C4-move-inherited`). A `move` method with a body in an `abstract` class can never run, since no object is exactly that class, and gets EN0459, `` `move` method of an abstract class can never run ``, with `` help: declare it `abstract` ``, as a warning, while an `abstract move` method compiles. When member lookup finds a method that the receiver's class inherits, typing records `Upcast` after the receiver's `AutoBorrow` or `Reborrow`, to the class that declares the method, as it does for `super.m()`, so MIR building reads only adjusts and the receiver reaches the call through a `Convert` that keeps its loans (section 5, `TESTING.md` `C21-inherited`); M2's `call_receiver` (`typeck/call.rs`) records it for every inherited method, and for an overloaded or generic method the call records it once the candidate it runs is chosen. An optional call `recv?.m()` is the exception: its `OptChain` holds no receiver, so MIR building adds the `Upcast` where it passes the payload as the receiver, as `call_receiver` would.

**Narrowing** is flow state inside `check_expr`, not a post-pass, because it changes types. A `FactSet = BTreeMap<Path, Fact>` follows control flow, where `Path` is a local or `this` plus field steps, and a `Fact` is non-null or, inside a `match` arm and its guard, the arm's enum case. Facts come from conditions and arms and die per SPEC 4.8 (N2), only on a visible change: an assignment, a move of the path, or a direct write to the path or a prefix. Every other reference made to a proper prefix of a fact's path, or to the path itself at its nullable type, is recorded with its span: a call argument, a receiver, a copy, a binding (destructuring included), a capture or a hidden argument. Typing cannot tell which of these write, because it never reads a contract, so phase 3 checks the records (section 6.7); a use whose expected type is the nullable type, or passing the path at a non-null `&T`, records nothing. `check_call` checks arguments before committing a receiver's narrowed type, and an assignment checks its target's operands, calls included, left to right, then, for Copy operands, a compound assignment's read of the old value, then its right-hand side, then its place's narrowed use, so kills run in O6 order, and a fact killed by an argument gives EN0401 with a note. A compound assignment to a place that is not Copy reads nothing early: its place's narrowed use comes right after its operands and before its right-hand side, since section 5 reserves the place there until the store, so its fact is read there, and the right-hand side cannot kill it unseen: a write of the place or of a prefix there is EN0707 against the reservation, so `if (o.s != null) { o.s += clear(&o); }` with a `clear` that writes `o` is EN0707 at `&o` (`TESTING.md` `SLB-07-narrow`). An assignment to a path, used as a value, is that path read after the store (SPEC 3.5), a copy of it for a Copy path and the path itself otherwise (section 5), so a test of it is a test of the path, as SPEC 4.8 creates the fact: `while ((line = readLine()) != null) { ... }` with `line: string | null` gives `line` a non-null fact in the loop body, as tsc does.

On loop entry, and for each arrow argument, a prescan of the AST collects the visible changes: assignment targets, compound-assignment targets, `++`/`--` targets, writes through `*` and moves of a path, including inside nested arrow bodies and spread operands (SPEC 4.8). It has no terms for borrows, receivers or bare uses, since a write through a reference shows up only in phase 3. The kill rules apply to everything collected before the loop condition, or before the arrow bodies of that call.

**`check_for_of`** runs in this order:

1. Type the source under the current facts.
2. Classify it (section 2.4).
3. Prescan the body only, so `[...p]` in the source has used its fact already.
4. Apply the kills to every fact except the source facts: those of each `NarrowedPayload(q, _)` in a `Path`'s `place`.
5. Check the body. Each kill in the flow state keeps its span, and each `continue` and each `break` of this loop, labelled or not, records its state. A `break outer` in an inner loop is a `break` of `outer` and not of the inner loop.
6. The source facts are not checked again at the back edges. The loop holds its source path (section 6.2), so a kill of a source fact on a way back to the head is already a phase 3 error: EN0706 for an assignment or a write, EN0709 for a move.
7. The state after the loop is the join of the head's exit edge with every recorded `break`: the intersection of their `FactSet`s. It is never the head's state alone, because source facts escape the kills of step 4, so a `break` path may have lost one. A read of a lost fact after the loop is EN0303 or EN0401 by its use, and never reaches a `CheckTag` panic.

A move is a kill, but a source fact escapes the prescan's kills, so `if (p != null) { for (const x of &p) { take(p); } }` passes phase 2, and in phase 3 the move conflicts with the loop's hold (EN0709). Without `&`, `for (const x of q) { take(q); }` takes `q` before the first turn, so `take(q)` is EN0601: `` `q` was used up by the loop ``.

A narrowed read becomes `NarrowedPayload`, which lowers to `CheckTag` in the run of checks directly before the projection, in the same block, so a narrowing bug panics and cannot read garbage.

**Exhaustiveness.** Arms bind nothing and do not nest, so each `match` is one column of arm values. Enum cases, `Ok`, `Err` and `null` are covered by membership. Numbers and strings always need `default`, so their literals and half-open ranges count only for redundancy, by interval cover; a string-literal union needs no `default` once every value is listed. A guarded arm covers nothing. Witnesses give EN0501 and redundancy EN0503.

**Const eval.** A small interpreter over HIR for SPEC 4.9, using checked arithmetic at target widths. Failure or a cycle is EN0412. A constant literal view at an annotated `&Slice<T>` or `&T[N]`, as in `public const PRIMES: &Slice<i64> = &[2, 3, 5, 7];`, is a `ConstExpr`, as a string literal at `&string` is: its value is the literal, which the defining module emits once as a read-only static in `.rodata`, laid out as its `T[N]` (section 7.7), with the top-level `const` mark (section 6.1), and the constant's value names that static, its address and static length, as a `&string` constant's names its bytes, so every reader, in any module, points at that one static and holds no loan (section 6.8), and `PRIMES[0] = 1;` in an importer is EN0608, naming the top-level `const` (`TESTING.md` `FV-07-const-view`). `sizeOf`, `alignOf` and `offsetOf` of a borrowing class or tuple are not constant: the form of a `&string` field or part follows permissions that phase 3 infers (section 6.7), and typing never reads a contract (N1), so in a `ConstExpr` they are EN0412, with the note that the type's layout depends on what its methods do with its references. In a body they fold at mono (section 2.6), after contracts exist. A top-level `assert` whose condition is constant is `ItemKind::StaticAssert`, EN0415 on failure; among a program's top-level statements, one whose condition is not constant is checked at run time.

**Closures in typeck.** An arrow is typed from its expected type: a closure parameter's, after all non-arrow arguments, or an annotated local's; otherwise from its own annotations, with its return type from its body (SPEC 5.5). A return type inferred from the body is never a C19 site, since C19 needs its target before it checks the operand: the returns unify as they are, with no wrapping and no EN0455, and `Err(e)` with no expected type takes `E` from its operand, `Failure` for a text, and a fresh `T`, as `Ok(x)` with none takes `Failure` for `E`. So `(s: &string) => { if (s.isEmpty()) { return Err("empty"); } return s.length(); }` is EN0401, and so is the same arrow with its two returns the other way round, each with `` help: annotate the result, `(s: &string): Result<isize> => ...`, or write `Ok(s.length())` ``, while the annotated form compiles. At a nullable closure parameter (`handler?: (k: u8) => void`) the expected type is the payload's, and the argument records `ArrowToClosure` then `WrapNull` (C12, then C06). A call of such a parameter types only where a fact proves it non-null (**Narrowing**, above), as in `if (handler != null) { handler(key); }`, and an unnarrowed call is the usual nullable error. The narrowing prescan runs first. Captures are computed from the typed arrow body and recorded for N2, and phase 3 gives each its mode (N4).

## 5. MIR construction

- **Evaluation order (O6).** Left to right. Call: receiver value operand, arguments left to right, then the receiver's auto-borrow last, so `a.push(a.length())` needs no two-phase borrow. An explicit `&` argument takes effect at its position. Assignment: the target's value operands first, left to right, an index and any call in the target included; then, for a compound assignment whose operands are Copy, the read of the old value, with its own `CheckBounds` and projection; then the right-hand side; then `CheckBounds` and the projection of the store. So `a[i] = i++` writes `a[0]`, `a[i] = shrink(&a)` and `a[i] += shrink(&a)` compute the store's address after the call, and `let x = [1]; x[0] += (x[0] = 5);` leaves `x[0]` at `6`, as in JavaScript. In `*slot(&xs) = val(&xs);` `slot` runs first, and the reference it returns is borrowed for writing while `val` reads `xs`, which is EN0708; `const v = val(&xs); *slot(&xs) = v;` compiles. The early read of the old value applies only to Copy operands. A compound assignment to a place that is not Copy, as `s += reset(&s)` on a `string`, which appends in place (SPEC 6), reads nothing early, since that read would move out of the place, or out of an array element in `names[0] += take(&names)`: after the target's operands come the place's `CheckBounds` and projection and `_p = &place [compound-target]`, then the right-hand side, then the operation in place through `_p`, for a string the append of section 7.7. Phase 3 makes `_p` a reservation, which turns exclusive at the store, as O6 takes a receiver's exclusive borrow last (section 6.2): from the target's operands to the store the right side may read the place, so on `s` holding `ab`, `s += "-".repeat(s.length());` and then `s += s.toUpperCase();` leave `ab--` and `ab--AB--`, as in Rust and Node, while a right side that changes the place, by a write or an exclusive borrow, is EN0707, at the `&s` of `reset(&s)` and at the `&names` of `take(&names)`, and one that moves it is EN0709. At the store `_p` turns exclusive, since the operation writes through it, so a loan on the place that the right side leaves live there is EN0708, as the part that `s.slice(0, 1)` holds in `s += s.slice(0, 1)` and the operand of `s += s` are. An element place follows the same rule, so `names[0] += pad(names.length());` compiles and prints `['x--', 'yz']` for `names` holding `x` and `yz`, where rustc rejects the twin (a named row of `corpus/rustc_disagreements.txt`). EN0707's help splits the statement, `const r = reset(&s); s += r;`, which compiles and prints `b!` (`TESTING.md` `S2-22-string`); EN0708's appends a copy of what the right side reads, `` help: append a copy: `s += s.slice(0, 1).toString();` ``, since a part would still hold `s` at the append (SPEC O6). `*grown(&s) += "!"` still appends in place, and no append ever reaches a string that the right side freed.
- **Reference copies (O7, N4).** Every use of a reference local `r` as a value lowers to `tmp = &*r [reborrow]`, so `const b = a` is `tmp = &*a; b = move tmp`. A user-visible reference local is never moved. Only the fresh reborrow temporary is moved, once, into its destination, and the verifier checks that any `Move` of a reference names a `Temp`. Phase 3 decides each reborrow by N4: a new exclusive loan on `*r` if the copy is write-live, else a new shared loan if `r` is, else no new loan, only what `r` holds. A nullable reference local is copied by one `Use(Copy)`, since `&*r` needs a present value, and phase 3 gives that copy the same choice on its payload, a new loan on `*(r as Present).0` normalised to root `r` with `through_ref`, so `const c: &i64[] | null = a;` with `c` written through, while `const e = &a[0];` under `a != null` is still used, is EN0708 at `&a[0]` (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-field-copy`). A reference read out of a reference field of a borrowing value or out of a reference part of a tuple, at any depth, nullable or not, and used as a value, a copy into a slot, an argument, a stored operand or a returned value, is a copy of that reference too, one `Use(Copy)` of the field or part, and phase 3 gives it N4's choice with the source counted as write-live, so it always takes a new loan on what the field points at, exclusive when the copy is write-live and shared otherwise, as an auto-borrow through the field does: a field is no slot, and nothing tracks whether it is written through elsewhere, so a copy with no loan would give the field's loans a second holder with no loan between them. Only a part of a tuple held directly in a local, a parameter or a temporary, whose parts N3 follows as slots of their own, takes N4's choice as a reference local does (section 6.2). So `const p = c.dst; const q = c.dst;` inside `Copier`, with `q` stored into a field that `Copier` writes through, is EN0706 at the second `c.dst`, and `const b2 = pair[0]; b2.push(9);` while `const x = &pair[0][0];` is still used is EN0706 at `pair[0]`, as `rustc`'s E0502 is (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-field-copy`). A copy of a borrowing tuple or a nullable one, `const p2 = pair` or `*t` through a reference, is one `Use(Copy)`, and phase 3 gives it the same choice for each reference part, nested parts included, by that part's write-liveness in the copy and in the source (section 6.2), so `const p2 = pair; const f = &p2[0][0]; pair[0].push(4);` with `f` used afterwards is EN0706 at the push. Whatever N4 gives, a copy is an access of what the reference it copies points at: `tmp = &*r` is a borrow access of `*r` even when it makes no new loan, and a copy of a nullable reference's payload or of a part of a borrowing tuple, alone or as a part of a whole tuple copy, that takes no new loan is checked the same way, as a shared borrow of `*(r as Present).0` or of what the part points at, so it meets a live exclusive loan there: with `let t: [&i64[], i32] = [&v, 1];`, `const a = t[0]; const b = t[0]; const e = &b[0]; a.push(3); a.push(4); console.log(*e);` is EN0708 at `const b = t[0];`, as it is at `const b = r;` when `const r = &v;` stands for `t`, where a copy that read only the part `t[0]` let `a`'s pushes reallocate `v` under `e` (SPEC 5.8 H39, `TESTING.md` `FV-02-part-copy`).
- **Reference conversions (C18, C21, C22).** Each `FixedToSlice`, `ArrayToSlice`, `Upcast`, `BoxToInterface` and `RefToInterface` adjust lowers to one `Rvalue::Convert` of that kind (section 2.5), after the borrow or reborrow its chain begins with, so `const s: &Slice<i64> = r;` with `r: &i64[]` is `tmp = &*r [reborrow]; s = Convert(ArrayToSlice, move tmp)`, and the receiver of an inherited method is `tmp = &c [auto-borrow]; tmp2 = Convert(Upcast, move tmp)`. Phase 3 gives the result its operand's loans and marks (section 6.2), so `r.push(4)` while `s` is still used is EN0706, as `rustc`'s E0502 is (`TESTING.md` `C18-loan`, `C21-inherited`, `C22-loan`). A spread's `extendCloned` operand gets its view the same way (below).
- **Parameter defaults (SPEC 4.14).** A default belongs to the callee's body, so N3, sources, stores, captures and moves all see it: `p: T = d` lowers as the body's first statements, `if (p was left out) { p = d; }`, in parameter order, in a constructor after its class's field initialisers and before its parameter-property assignments (**Construction**, below), each an initialisation of `p` and never EN0613, where each test reads a hidden `isize` argument, the number of parameters the call supplied. A call that leaves parameters out passes the arguments supplied, then the hidden ones, then that number, so phase 3 maps the callee's contract to them: each permission to the argument or reference part it is of, an omitted parameter's dropped, since it holds no loan of the caller's, and the sources, through sets and stores renumbered (section 6.2). A direct call passes that number, a `Virtual` call too, and `FnToPtr` and `FnAsClosure` reach the function through an entry that passes the full number, since an `fn` value takes every parameter. A closure parameter defaulted to an arrow holds that arrow's captures, as a local holding an arrow does (SPEC 5.5). So with `visit(xs: &i64[], step: () => void = () => { xs.push(0); })`, `xs` is Write, `visit(&v)` on a `const v` is EN0608, and `const head = xs.first();` before `step()` is EN0715, `` `xs` is locked while `step` will still be used ``; with `n: isize = grow(xs)` for a `grow` that pushes, `xs` is Write as well.
- **Declarations.** `let x: T;` lowers to an assignment of `T`'s default when `T` has one (SPEC O3), so such a local is initialised from its declaration; a local of a type with no default stays uninitialised for the init dataflow (EN0602).
- **Construction (SPEC 4.3).** `new D(args)` lowers into its destination: for a class with a hidden pointer, `SetTable` first writes `D`'s own table, so the object is a `D` before any field is set, and then `D`'s constructor runs on it as a direct call. A constructor takes the object as its first argument, `_1: &D`, which is `this`, exactly as a method takes a `&` or `move` receiver (`Tcx::takes_this`, true for every constructor, whose `FnSig.receiver` stays `None`; section 2.5), so `new D(a)` is `_r = &dest [auto-borrow]; _v = D::#ctor(move _r, a)`, the borrow last as a receiver's (O6), init dataflow exempts that borrow of the uninitialised destination and counts the call as initialising it, and `this` is index 0 of the constructor's contract, as in the `(1, 0)` store of `Lexer`'s (section 6.6). A constructor runs one prologue, in this order: its class's field initialisers and type defaults, in declaration order, for its own fields with a default and for each inherited field it overrides, with the new default, none of which uses `this` or a parameter; then its parameters' defaults, in parameter order (**Parameter defaults**, above); then its parameter-property assignments; then its written body, up to `super(...)` in a class with a base. So a parameter property with a default stores the default when its argument is left out: with `public constructor(public name: string = "anon", public nick?: string) {}`, `new Animal()` holds `anon` and `null`, and `new Animal("Rex")` holds `Rex` and `null` (`TESTING.md` `C4-pprop-default`). In a class with a base, everything before `super(...)` may only assign the class's own fields, and must assign each one without a default (EN0610, section 6.1), so a parameter default there that reads `this` or calls a method is EN0610, as it is in any class while a field without a default is still unassigned; `super(args)` is then a direct call of the base's constructor on the same object, which builds the base part the same way. `super(...)` runs exactly once on every path that returns normally, and only as a statement of the constructor's own body in a class with a base, never inside an arrow or a local function: init dataflow tracks it as a pseudo-path (section 6.1), and EN0619 reports a `return` or a closing brace that it may not have reached, a `super(...)` that may already have run, and one inside an arrow or a local function of the constructor (elsewhere it is phase 2's EN0219), while a path that ends in a panic needs none. A chain therefore runs level by level, most derived first: `Dog`'s initialisers and its assignments before `super(...)`, then `Animal`'s initialisers and body, then the rest of `Dog`'s body. A subclass with no constructor, of a base that has one, gets a synthesised one that runs its initialisers and passes its arguments on to the base's. It has no `DefId` and is inlined wherever it is reached: `new B(args)` calls the base's constructor, which sema gives the `new`, and after the arguments, before the receiver's borrow, it stores the initialisers and type defaults of each class from `B` up to, not including, the class whose constructor runs, most derived first, as plain stores, a field that a class below already stored skipped, and then passes `convert(upcast, move _r)` as `this`, so no copy of the base's parameter defaults is needed; init dataflow exempts the inlined stores and the borrow and counts the call as initialising `dest`, as for any `new`, and phase 3 tells this upcast of `&dest [auto-borrow]` from the `super(...)` upcast of `&*_1 [reborrow]`. A `super(args)` in a class `C` whose direct base has no constructor does the same into `*_1` before its upcast, each store behind `Overridden(f)` and ending in a `FieldInit` when `C` can be extended, the fields `C` overrides skipped. Mono takes the override set of the constructor's instance from the upcast's source class: the fields that the classes from it up to, not including, the constructor's class override, added for a `super(...)` to the set of the instance making the call. Each class of a chain in which no class has a constructor has an implicit `public` one with no parameters, which runs its initialisers and type defaults, with the override set of its instance, and then its base's, as `new Counter()` does, so a subclass that declares a constructor over such a chain calls `super()` under the same exactly-once rule, and `super(args)` with arguments is EN0449. Such a `super()` leaves unset each inherited field with no default, by an initialiser or a type default in its own class or by an override in a class down to the subclass, and the subclass's constructor assigns it after `super()`, as TypeScript's `super(); this.owner = o;` does, but checked, so no `!` is needed: before `super()` it may assign only its own fields (EN0610), and the rule that every field without a default is assigned covers such an inherited field too, so reading `this`, calling a method, passing `this`, or reaching a `return` or the closing brace before it is assigned is EN0610, `` `owner` is not set ``, with `help: assign this.owner after super()` (section 6.1). That is sound because a base with no constructor runs none of its own code inside `super()`, and its initialisers cannot use `this`, so nothing sees the field unset. With `public class Shape { protected color: string = "red"; public owner: Person; }`, whose `describe()` returns `` `${this.color} shape of ${this.owner.name}` ``, and `public class Circle extends Shape { public constructor(public r: i64, o: Person) { super(); this.owner = o; } }`, `console.log(new Circle(2, new Person("Ada")).describe());` prints `red shape of Ada`, and without `this.owner = o;` it is EN0610 at the closing brace (`TESTING.md` `C4-ctorless-base`). Where a class below may override such a field with a default, the field starts maybe-initialised in the subclass's constructor, with its drop flag set from `Overridden(field)`, as a field with no default does in its own class's constructor (below), so the D3 drop of `this.owner = o` exists exactly in the instances where an override stored a value. The initialiser of a field that a class below overrides is skipped, so each field is stored once, with the most derived default: a constructor of a class that can be extended takes, at compile time, the set of fields of its chain that the classes below it override, which each `super(...)` passes on with its own class's overrides added, and mono makes one instance per set reached, as for a type argument, the empty set for a plain `new` of a class with a constructor of its own (`Instance.overrides`, section 2.6). In such a constructor each initialiser or type default ends in a `FieldInit { field, value }` (section 2.5), and the blocks that compute its value are entered through a `Switch` on the mono constant `Overridden(field)`, which mono folds from the instance's set, as it folds `Len` on `Fixed`, so an instance whose set holds the field neither runs the initialiser nor stores it. An implicit constructor takes such a set in a `final` class too, since a named form's fill function adds to it the fields the form names (below), so its initialisers and type defaults end in `FieldInit` as well. Init dataflow counts the field as initialised where the two edges of that `Switch` join, the edge that skips the `FieldInit` included, which is sound because in an instance that skips it a class below stored the field before its own `super(...)`, or the named form whose fill function called an implicit constructor stores it right after that call, and an implicit constructor reads no field (section 6.1). A field with no default of its own that a class below may override, one that is not `private` in a class that can be extended, starts maybe-initialised in its class's constructor, with a drop flag set from `Overridden(field)`, so the D3 drop of the constructor's assignment to it exists exactly in the instances where an override stored a value: with `Animal` assigning `owner` in its constructor and `protected override owner: Person = new Person("nobody");` in `Dog`, `new Dog(new Person("Ada"))` drops `nobody` at that assignment and leaks nothing (`TESTING.md` `C4-override-nodefault`). An assignment by a base's constructor body, a parameter property included, comes later and wins, as any assignment does (D3). So a call through `this`, or printing `this`, in `Animal`'s constructor runs `Dog`'s code and reads only fields already set, and with `protected override sound: string = "woof";` in `Dog`, `Animal`'s constructor already reads `woof` (`TESTING.md` `S2-02-ctor` for the order, `Q32-field-override` for `woof`). The named form (`NewNamed`), which only a class of a chain with no constructor takes (EN0447), leaves every field it does not name to the class's own module (SPEC 4.3), as a parameter's default belongs to its callee: it evaluates its named values left to right (O6), then calls, on its destination, the class's `NamedFill` shim for its set of left-out fields, `default_from`, which init dataflow counts as initialising exactly those fields, and then moves each named value into its field, an initialisation, as in a constructor. `NamedFill` first writes the class's table, when the class has a hidden pointer, as `new` does, and then calls the class's implicit constructor (above) at the instance whose override set is the fields the form names, so each left-out field gets its most derived default, each named one is skipped, and a field with no default, which the form must name (EN0433), is never stored there (SPEC 4.3); mono makes one instance of each per set reached, as for an override set (section 2.6). So `new Dog { name: "Rex" }` has `woof` too, and every default stays in the stored code of the module that declares it, a base's in its base's module, `private` fields included: an importer's MIR names only the shim and the fields it writes itself, so no field default and no `private` field appears there, the visibility check on load passes a genuine file (section 2.5, `TESTING.md` `MD3-01-named`), and editing `public retries: i64 = 3;` to 5 in `lib` changes only `lib`'s stored code, so a warm build prints `5` as a cold one does, and so does a `mid` with no source, with no EN0315 (`MD3-03-default`).
- **Scopes and drops.** A scope stack with one entry per block, plus one temporary scope for each boundary in the D2 table: each condition of `if`, `while`, `do`, `for` and ternary, each `for` step, each right operand of `&&` and `||`, and each full statement. `match` subject and `for...of` source temporaries belong to the whole statement. Each exit (`break`, `continue`, `return`, `?`, fall-through) emits `Drop` and `StorageDead` for every scope crossed, in reverse, with temporaries before the locals of their block (D2). A `move` method owns its object, so when its class needs a drop, its body scope schedules `Drop(*_1)` before its by-value parameters, and every exit drops what the body leaves of `*this` after them; after a whole move, as `return this`, nothing of `*_1` may be initialised, so that `Drop` is no access (section 6.2), and elaboration removes it.
- **Mixed integer comparisons** (SPEC 4.6) compare true values: both operands widen to a type that holds both ranges, and where none exists, a signed 64-bit value against an unsigned 64-bit one, the sign is tested first.
- **Assignment (D3).** `tmp = v; Drop(x); x = tmp;`. An assignment to a Copy place used as a value, or `++`/`--`, also leaves its value in a temporary: the assigned value, the new value for a prefix form, the old one for a postfix form; a reference place copies its reference (O7). For a place that is not Copy, a string, an array or an object, no temporary is made, since a copy would be a second owner and `tmp` has already moved into `x`: the value operand is the place `x` itself, read after the store, so `x` stays the only owner (SPEC O1). A use that does not keep it, a comparison, `== null` or `!= null`, an argument of `console.log`, a template hole, an operator or a `&` argument, borrows `x` as it would borrow `x` written there by name, so `console.log(s += "!");` prints `a!` for `s` holding `a` and leaves `a!` in `s`, and `while ((line = readLine()) != null)` over a `string | null` tests `line` itself (section 4). A use that would keep the value, a binding initialiser as in `const t = (s += "?");`, the right side of another assignment as in `a = b = s2;`, a by-value argument, a `return`, an element or field of a literal or a capture by value, would take it from `x`, which still owns it, so it is EN0621 in phase 3, `an assignment's value can't be kept`, labelled `` `s += "?"` is still owned by `s` ``, with `` help: assign first, then copy: `s += "?"; const t = s.clone();` ``, or, for a chain, `` help: assign one at a time: `b = s2; a = b.clone();` ``: mirbuild lowers such a use as a `Move` of `x` that it records as the assignment's value, and the move check reports EN0621 at it instead of moving `x`, so `x` stays initialised (section 6.1, `TESTING.md` `SLB-01-value`). A Copy place keeps its copy, so `a = b = 0;` sets both (above).
- **Exact-class checks.** The check is defined by the event, not by the syntax: every copy or move of a whole object of a class type, reached through a `Deref` of a reference or a `Box`, `this` included, and every write of such a whole object, gets a `CheckClass` on the object, in the run of checks directly before what moves it. That is `*a = v` before the old value's `Drop` (`Assign`); a copy out (`CopyOut`) by C05 (`ReadThrough`) or C08 (`ReadThroughNullable`), by an explicit `*r` or `*b` in a value position, by a ternary or `??` arm that reads one, or into the Copy receiver of a `move` method called through a reference or a `Box`; the entry to a `move` method of a class `A` that can be extended, own or inherited, since the method takes the whole object as `this`, moves out of it as an `A` and disposes of the rest with `A`'s glue (`MoveThis`, the first statement of the body); and the object of a call to `unbox`, `clone`, `mem.replace` or `mem.swap` (both objects for `mem.swap`, and `*b` for a `Box<A>`'s `clone`). For `unbox` and `clone` it ends the run of checks directly before the block's `Call`; for `mem.replace` and `mem.swap` each object's check sits directly before the borrow that passes that object, an explicit `&`, an auto-borrow or the reborrow of a reference value, since that borrow is exclusive and reserves the object to the call, so a check after it would read the object under its own loan. It sits in the caller, so the panic names the user's line, except that `MoveThis` sits at the entry of the `move` method's own body, which every call reaches, a call on `this` from another method included; after it, `this` is exactly `A`, so a whole move of `this`, as `return this`, needs no check of its own. Mirbuild emits it wherever the object, reached through a `Deref` of a reference or a `Box`, has a class type or a type parameter, because a generic body does not know its `T`, and mono erases every one whose type is not a class that can be extended (section 2.6). Its `via` is `Box` when the place's last `Deref` goes through a `Box` and `Ref` when it goes through a reference, `this` included. It reads the object's hidden method-table pointer and compares it with `A`'s table, and the panic names the operation by the word SPEC 9.1 gives it (`` `unbox` ``, `assignment` for `Assign`, `copy` for `CopyOut`, the method's name for `MoveThis`, `` `mem.replace` ``, `` `mem.swap` ``, `` `clone` ``), `A`, what holds the object, by `via`, and the class that the object's table records: `` `unbox` expected an `Animal`, but the box holds a `Dog` ``, through an `&A` `` `mem.swap` expected an `Animal`, but the reference points at a `Dog` ``. A `MoveThis` check stays in every `move` method of a class that can be extended, but outside generic code and per-class copies phase 2 already rejects each call that would enter an inherited one with an object of a subclass (EN0458, section 4), so only a generic instance or a per-class copy can reach it with one: for `public move into(): Animal { return this; }` inherited by `Dog`, `rex.into()` is EN0458, while `function f<T extends Animal>(x: T): Animal { return x.into(); }` called with `rex` panics `` `into` expected an `Animal`, but the reference points at a `Dog` `` on entry to `into`, and called with `new Animal("Generic")` returns it (SPEC 9.1, `TESTING.md` `S2-03-into` and `S2-03-copy`).
- **`?`.** `Switch` on the tag; the error arm moves the payload into `_0` as `Err` or `null` and exits through scheduled drops. `TryKind::ResultIntoFailure` first turns the error into a `Failure` that keeps its text (SPEC 6), and then drops the error value. For an enum with no written `fmt` the conversion switches on the tag: a case with no fields takes its text, or its qualified name, from a read-only table as a static descriptor (`cap == 0`), with no allocation, and a case with fields calls the `alloc` lang item `failure_from_display`, which prints the value through its `Display` into a buffer `Formatter`. An `implements Error` class always calls it, and so does an enum that writes `fmt`, for every case, since an error's text is what it prints: with an `AgeError` whose `fmt` writes `invalid age`, `parse(s)?` into `Result<i64>` gives the text `invalid age`, never a case's text (`TESTING.md` `V-ME-07-fmt`). Whether any case has fields, and whether the enum writes `fmt`, are properties of the type, so on a target with no heap the call, and EN0820 with it, comes from the type and not from the case a run produces (section 2.6). In a generic body whose error type is a type parameter, typing picks `ResultIntoFailure` (and `ErrForm::IntoFailure` for `Err(e)`), and mono chooses the conversion per instance from the concrete `E`: where `E` is `Failure`, `?` and `Err(e)` move it as for an identical `E` (`TryKind::Result`, `ErrForm::Value`), with no call to `failure_from_display`, so `lift<u8, Failure>` stays static where a direct `ready()?` is (section 2.6).
- **`Err`.** `Callee::Form(Err(form))` builds the `Err` case at the expected `E` from its operand `x`. `ErrForm::Value` moves `x`. `Text` makes a `Failure` from a fixed text as a static descriptor (`cap == 0`), or from a template through an `alloc` buffer `Formatter`, or, at a literal-union `E`, is that union's value. `TextMove` moves a `string`'s three words into the `Failure`, with no copy, so `Err(msg)` with `let msg: string` keeps the text it built and `msg` is moved afterwards; `TextCopy` copies a `&string`'s bytes into a new `string` through `alloc`, as `Err(why)` with `why: &string` does, and as `Err(&this.name)` does for a field; `Err(this.name)` itself is a move out of `this`, EN0603 (section 4). `IntoFailure` converts as `ResultIntoFailure` does, so `Err(AgeError.NotANumber)` in a `Result<i64>` function gives the text `not a number` when `AgeError` writes no `fmt`, and the text its `fmt` writes when it does; when `x` is a case of an enum with no written `fmt`, written in place, only that case's conversion is built, so `Err(DiskError.Timeout)` allocates nothing.
- **Returns in a `Result` function.** `return v` with `v: T` in a function returning `Result<T, E>` carries `WrapOk` and builds `Ok { value: v }`, and falling off the end of a `Result<void, E>` body returns `Ok()`, as `return;` in such a function or arrow does (SPEC 6).
- **`match`.** `match` never takes its subject. A place subject is used where it is; an rvalue subject goes into a statement temporary. Arms are tried top to bottom: enum cases, `Ok`, `Err` and `null` by a `Switch` on the tag, literals and ranges by compares, `match (true)` arms by evaluating each condition in turn, and a guard after its arm's values match. Inside an arm, reads of the subject's fields are narrowed reads, `NarrowedPayload` of the arm's case, lowered like any other narrowing, and an arm that moves a field moves it as anywhere else. How an arm reads the fields of an rvalue subject, such as `match (make())`, is not designed yet, and SPEC does not say. An impossible default is `Unreachable`.
- **Destructuring.** A `Destructure` of a reference gives each binding a reference to its part, `Ref [destructure]`; of an owned value, it copies `Copy` parts and moves the others out, so the source is partly moved afterwards (SPEC 5.6). The rule applies recursively at every level of a nested pattern. A destructuring assignment (`Pat::Place`), as `[a, b] = [b, a];` or `[this.a, this.b] = [x, y];`, evaluates its right side into a temporary, then assigns each part from it to its place, in source order, each as an ordinary `Assign`, so a part whose place lies inside a borrowing pointee is a write inside that pointee, which goes through `store`, rule (e), the read-set check and N3's per-part tracking (section 6.2, `TESTING.md` `D9-store`).
- **`for...of`.** Mirbuild only matches `ForSource` (section 2.4). `Path` and every `Once` in `Ref` mode lower to one shape, with no special case for `T[N]`, because mono folds `Len` on `Fixed`:

  ```text
  bbP:    { StorageLive(_i); _i = const 0; goto bbH; }
  bbH:    { [CheckTag run]; _n = Len(S); _c = Lt(copy _i, copy _n); switch _c [0: bbX, otherwise: bbB]; }
  bbB:    { StorageLive(x); CheckBounds(copy _i, copy _n); [CheckTag run];
            x = &S[_i] [for-of-element]; _i = Add(copy _i, const 1); goto bbBody; }
  bbBody: ... { StorageDead(x); goto bbH; }
  ```

  - `_i` and `_n` are `isize` locals of kind `HiddenLoop(Index)` and `HiddenLoop(Length)`. `Add` is the wrapping `Binary`, because `_i < _n <= isize.MAX`, and it sits in the bind block, so every back edge, `continue` included, already carries the next index when the safe-point poll runs. `continue` lands on `bbH` after `StorageDead(x)`, and that per-turn reset is why a `const` binding never meets EN0613. A destructuring binding binds its parts from `x`.
  - `S` is the `Path`'s place. For a `Path`, the loop's hold on its source (SPEC 5.6) is a phantom slot that exists only in the borrow checker (section 6.2). It has no MIR local and no run-time storage, so the head still reads `S` every turn, and at a back edge the loop itself holds only `_i` (`DEV_RUNTIME.md` section 6).
  - For `Once` it is `*_src`, with `_src` of kind `HiddenLoop(Source)` set before `bbP`: `_src = &place [for-of-hidden]` for a place, `_src = value` for a reference-typed rvalue. The head keeps `_src` live, so its loans last for the whole loop.
