Without this, with `if (n == 0) { stale = x; break; }` in the first run of a loop over `rows.slice(0)` inside a `while (n < 2)`, `const v = stale ?? x; return [a, v];` in a later turn of the second run would be decided by the site loans of `a` and `x` alone, an earlier turn against this one, though `stale` and `a` are both `rows[0]`; with it, `stale` holds the receiver's loan and `rows`'s key plainly, so `v` does too, and the pair is read-only (`TESTING.md` `TK-03-stale-join`). Only N6's part test reads the marks (section 6.7, stage 4), and it compares pair by pair: the pair formed by one key that both parts hold only via bind l is decided by their loans of l's bind site, by `loans_overlap`, and every other pair is compared by path, so a loop over a view of a parameter, `for (const x of xs.slice(1))`, whose `_src` holds the receiver's loan and `xs`'s key, or over a copied reference, `const r = xs; for (const x of r)`, gives a writable pair from two turns of one run, as a loop over `xs` itself does (`TESTING.md` `TK-03-tail`, `TK-03-copied-ref`). Leaving any key out of the comparison would be unsound: `a = &xs[1]` holds a plain loan on `xs[*]` and `xs`'s key plainly, and `x` from `for (const x of xs.slice(1))` may be the same element, so that pair stays read-only (`TK-03-tail-mixed`), and so does one where `a` starts as `&xs[2]` and takes `x` only on some paths, `if (c) { a = x; }`, since `a`'s plain loan on `xs[*]` still meets, by path, the receiver loan that `x` holds via the bind (`TK-03-tail-mixed-join`).

**Covered keys (N6).** Beside its bind mark, each `Param(i)`, `Fields(i)` or `RFields(i)` key in a slot is *covered* or *bare*, and only N6's part test reads which (section 6.7, stage 4). A site loan *crosses* `Param(i)` when its path is rooted at parameter i with `through_ref`, and crosses `Fields(i)` and `RFields(i)` only when it is so rooted and its first step is `Step::Fields`, so a loan on an owned field, `&this.own`, crosses `Param(0)` but not `Fields(0)`. Keys enter bare, at entry. A row that adds a new loan L that stays in `held(d)`, a `Ref`, a copy out of a field or part or a copy of a nullable reference, makes covered, in `held(d)`, each key there that L crosses; every other row passes each key's bit on as it passes its mark, a `Ref` with no new loan, `Use`, `Convert`, an `Aggregate`, a call result, a store and the renames included, and wherever two copies of one key meet in one slot, by any row, a bare copy makes it bare, as for the marks. So a key is covered only where every way it reached the slot came with a loan through parameter i that the slot still holds. Repointing a `let` parameter removes every loan that crossed its keys, so `kill` makes them bare in every slot (above). A flat set cannot say this on its own: with `const s = p;`, which takes no loan, `const second = c ? &p.ys : &s.xs;` holds the loan on `p.ys`, which crosses `Param(p)`, beside `Param(p)` bare from `s`, which could stand for `p.xs`, and reading the loan as covering it made `[&p.xs, second]` a writable pair of one array (`TESTING.md` `TK-01-copy-join`); so did `c ? p : &p.kids[0]` returned beside `&p.val` (`TK-01-pick-param`), a loop's binding of a copied reference joined with a borrow through the parameter (`TK-03-copied-ref-join`), a literal holding a copy of the parameter (`TK-03-whole-join`), a reference field copied out (`TK-01-field-copy-join`, `TK-03-field-join`), and a borrow kept from before a `let` parameter was repointed (`TK-01-repoint`).

**Liveness: backward.** A use is any occurrence of a local as an operand or as the base of a place, including `*r = v`. A definition is a whole-local assignment, and, for a loop's phantom slot, the hold its pre-header takes. `StorageDead` is never a use. `Drop` of a ref-carrying local is a use only when a destructor runs on it or on a borrowing value inside it, a nullable field's included (O10, I5): a reference has none, and a borrowing value whose drop only frees owned fields, such as a `Tagged` with a `string` label, ends its loans at its last use. `V` locals are ignored, since they hold nothing. A loop's phantom slot is defined at its loop's pre-header and used at its head, and nowhere else. A parameter's phantom slot `caller(m)` is used at every `Return`, since the caller's value outlives the call: a loan stored there stays live to the end of the body, so storing a reference to a local in `this` is EN0711 at the local's `StorageDead`.

**Live loans at point P:** the union of `held_P(s)` over slots `s` live at P.

**Access check.** Each statement performs its reads with liveness before the statement, and its write with liveness after the statement minus the destination. So `x = f(&x)` is accepted when the result holds nothing, and `x = *r` with `r` dead afterwards is accepted. This two-stage rule is the "mid-point" of NLL. A scope `Drop` of a path nothing of which may be initialised there by init dataflow, a definitely moved path such as `*_1` after a `move` method's `return this` (section 5), drops nothing, so it is no access, and elaboration removes it (section 2.6): a `move` method whose class needs a drop, as one with a `string` field does, and that ends in `return this` while what it returns holds a loan, gets no EN0710 from that `Drop`.

```text
overlap(loan, access_path, access_kind):
  shallow = access_kind in {StorageDead, Drop of a ref-carrying local on which no destructor runs,
                            reassign of a ref-carrying local}
  if shallow and loan.path.through_ref and loan.path.root == access_path.root: return false
  if loan is a hold's crossing loan and access_path goes on through the reference at loan.path: return false
                            // it keeps that reference from changing, not what the reference reaches (N9, above)
  return prefix(loan.path, access_path) or prefix(access_path, loan.path)

loans_overlap(a, b):    // one loan against another, as N6's part test compares them (section 6.7), never an access
  if a's key is Site(l, s) and b's is Site(l, t), for one for...of bind site l:
      return {s, t} != {This, EarlierThisRun}    // two turns of one run bind different elements
  return prefix(a.path, b.path) or prefix(b.path, a.path)
```

`[*]` matches itself. The exceptions are the turn keys (above): the skip at a loop's own head and bind accesses, and, between two loans, a `This` key against an `EarlierThisRun` key of one bind site. Different fields are disjoint. `Step::Fields` is disjoint from `Field(f)` of a reference field: a token reached through the field and a write of the field itself never overlap, since repointing `this.src` never touches its old pointee, so `next` may narrow `this.src` while a token is live. A loan's own holder needs no exemption: an access through a holder `h`, `*h` or a reference field of a borrowing `h`, is rooted at `h` with `through_ref`, and a loan `h` holds on another path does not overlap it, while an access to `h`'s own place, a move or a write of `lx`, is checked against every live loan, including one that `lx` holds on itself (SPEC 5.8 H05). So is a drop of `lx` that runs a destructor, at its scope end, and the store of an assignment whose `DropOrigin::Assign` drop of the old value runs one: that drop uses what `lx` holds on its own parts, so `lx`'s own slot meets it, though `lx` is not live after it (O10, O11). `Discriminant(x)` is a read of `x`, so a live exclusive loan on a field of one of `x`'s cases conflicts with matching `x` again. Then the O11 table:

| Access | Live shared | Live exclusive |
|---|---|---|
| Read, Copy, shared borrow | ok | EN0708 |
| Write, exclusive borrow | EN0706 | EN0707 |
| Move | EN0709 | EN0709 |
| Drop, `StorageDead` | EN0711 (EN0712 if the root is a `Temp`) | same |

A loop's `Hold` is the one exception: an access that changes the held source, a write or an exclusive borrow other than another loop's hold, head or bind, is EN0706 against it in both columns (SPEC 5.6). Another loop's hold, head or bind that meets a `Hold` reports by mode, so `for (const a of xs) { for (const b of xs) { *b = 1; } *a = 2; }` over `xs: &Slice<i64>` is EN0707 at the inner source. Calling a closure local is an exclusive use of that local, so a capture or `ClosureReborrow` of it conflicts and reports EN0718. Any other conflict in which the live loan or the borrow being checked has origin `ClosureCapture` reports EN0715, which is how `both(() => { *r += 1; }, r)` is EN0715 in either argument order (section 6.4).

**One report per mistake.** `DiagCtx` dedupes on code and span, which is not enough when one mistake gives two codes, so:

- Conflicts are keyed on (loan, access span). A `for...of` loop's hold at the pre-header, its head (its `CheckTag` run and `Len`) and its bind (its checks and `Ref`) all carry the source's span; when two of them meet the same loan the `Ref` wins, then the hold, which gives EN0706, EN0707 or EN0708 by mode, and never a second code. With `let keep: &i64 | null = null;` and a `use` that writes through its parameter, so that every bind is exclusive, `for (const x of &q) { if (c) { keep = x; } } for (const y of &q) { console.log(y); } use(keep);` is one EN0708 at the second loop's source, where its hold, `Len` and `Ref` read `q` while `keep` may still write into it. The first loop alone compiles, since its own head and bind skip the binds of its earlier turns (turn keys, above).
- Several loans at one access are tried in a fixed order and the first is kept: a loop's `Hold` first, then sites in `LoanId` order. So a change of a held source always reports the hold, as EN0706 whatever the hold's mode (a move is EN0709), with the `for...of` wording of SPEC 14 G08 and G21, and a `Once` source reports its `ForOfHidden` loan and not the element's.
- At one call, the read-set check of its stores (EN0616) is reported only where no borrow the call takes, an argument's or its receiver's, conflicted: a conflicting argument already names the aliasing that the check would, so `const p = w.dst; w.retarget(p);` is one EN0706 at the call; the call's stores still apply, so `w` now holds the loan through `w` that `p` held, and each later exclusive borrow of `w` is EN0706 too (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-upcast-local`).
- D3's `Drop` has `DropOrigin::Assign` and no access check of its own, since the store right after it reports EN0706 or EN0707, against the old value's own slot too when a destructor runs on it (H05, above). Nothing runs between the two and a `Drop` is no use of a reference, so both see the same live loans. `for (const x of &q) { q = []; console.log(x); }` is one EN0706.
- A marked argument passed to a parameter that writes, other than a receiver, is reported once, by its read-only mark (section 6.1), and a conflict of mode, EN0706, EN0707 or EN0708, at its borrow, or with the loan that borrow makes, is not reported again: the exclusive loan is the same mistake, so `const data: i64[] = [1]; const e = &data[0]; const g = new Grower(&data); console.log(*e);` with `FV-01-ctor`'s `Grower` is one EN0608 at `&data`, with no EN0706 there, and `FV-05-nested`'s `new Pad(&a0, &k)` over a `const k` is one EN0608 at `&k`, with no EN0708 at the later `&k[0]`. A conflict with any other loan is still reported, as is a move or a drop under that loan, a change of a loop's held source, a mistake of its own, and a receiver's conflict: `const q = [1]; for (const x of &q) { q.push(2); }` is EN0614 and EN0706. The mark error that silenced a conflict carries no edit, since making the root writable leaves the conflict.
- A rebinding of a captured reference inside an arrow, `r = x` (EN0616, section 6.4), is its one report: the store changes only the environment's copy of `r`, so it is no write through the arrow's environment, and calling the arrow borrows nothing for writing on its account, so `let a: i64 = 1; let r: &i64 = &a; const f = (x: &i64): void => { r = x; }; f(&a);` is one EN0616, with no EN0708 at `f(&a)`, while an arrow that also writes `*r` still makes that call EN0708.

### 6.3 R3

**Sources.** A function with a body and no `unsafe` gets its `RetSources` from the body, with its contract (section 6.7): the reference parameters, `this` included, whose `Param(i)`, `Fields(i)` or `RFields(i)` keys reach `held(_0)` at some `Return`. A source whose `Fields(i)` key reaches `held(_0)` and whose `Param(i)` key never does goes in `through_fields`, but only when the class of the borrowing value behind `i` never writes through its reference fields (its flag, section 6.7): `Lexer.next` returns `this.src.slice(...)`, which holds `Fields(0)` and not `Param(0)`, and `Lexer` only reads through `src`. Otherwise `i` stays an ordinary source, and the result holds the argument's own loan, exclusive when the result is write-live, as Rust ties a result to `&mut self`: with `Holder.first()` returning `&this.v[0]` and `reset()` writing `*this.v`, a token from `first` keeps `h` borrowed, so `h.reset()` while it is live is EN0706. A class whose method hands out a writable result reached through its fields has the flag set, so a `through_fields` result is always read-only, which is the condition Rust's `-> Option<&'a str>` model needs: SPEC 5.4's "the result is not write-live" holds wherever `through_fields` applies, since writing through such a result is EN0616. That condition concerns a reference result only: a by-value borrowing result, as `public rest(): Lexer { return new Lexer(this.src.slice(this.pos, this.src.length())); }` returns, gets `through_fields` whatever the caller does with it, since a write through it goes through its own class's methods, which that class's flag and rule (e) govern. A source whose `RFields(i)` key reaches `held(_0)` and whose `Param(i)` and `Fields(i)` keys never do reaches the value behind `i` only through its fields declared `readonly &`, and goes in `through_read` whatever the flag: the result holds `rfields(args[i])`, the value's read set (section 6.2), and no loan on the value, as Rust's `fn peek(&self) -> &'a [u8]` holds the field's lifetime and not `self`'s. So a `Copier` whose `peek()` returns `this.src.slice(this.pos, this.pos + 1)` hands out a token that a `step()` writing through `dst` leaves valid, and `const a = c.peek(); c.step(); c.step(); const b = c.peek(); console.log(a, b);` prints `[7] [9]` (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-copier`). Such a reference result is read-only, by stage 4, whatever marks the body's flows carry (section 6.7), and a borrowing value returned by value keeps those loans only in fields that nothing writes through, by rule (e), so it stays writable (`TESTING.md` `BC-08-readonly`); a result reached from any other reference field keeps the rules above: a `last()` returning `this.dst.slice(0, 1)` holds `Fields(0)`, so with the flag set it holds the object, and `const d = c.last(); c.step(); console.log(d);` is EN0706. The parameter still counts as a source for the argument's mode (N8) and for N3's rule (d); only what the result holds changes. In a `move` method the object is local to the call (SPEC 5.7 D4), so only `Fields(0)` or `RFields(0)` may reach the result (the table below). For an Own `this`, `through_fields` changes nothing at the call: the object moves into the call and holds no loan on itself, so either way the result holds what its fields held. Where no body can be read (interface members, abstract methods, `fn` values, `declare`) or trusted (a body containing `unsafe`, where `&*p` holds no loan), SPEC 5.4's signature defaults apply: `this` for a method, else all reference parameters, else `NoLoans`. A method's default is `this`, so `map.get(&key)` never holds `key`. A reference return with nothing to borrow from has `NoLoans`; its definition check is what fires, with EN0710 or EN0712. Signature defaults have no `through_fields` or `through_read`.

**Overridable methods.** An overridable method is any method of a class that isn't `final` other than a `static` or a `private` one, or one compiled once per type, with type parameters of its own, a by-value interface parameter or an object-type parameter: a `private` method is invisible to subclasses, so nothing overrides it and it needs no table slot, and one compiled once per type has no single body for a slot to name, so overriding it is EN0460 (section 4). An override may return into the object or into whatever the base body returns into, so an overridable method's declared sources are its base body's sources plus `this`, and its declared `through_fields` and `through_read` are its base body's without `this`. A call of an overridable method is `Virtual` exactly when the receiver place's last step dereferences a `&C` or `Box<C>` of a class `C` that isn't `final`, or the receiver is `this` inside such a class, as in `this.m()`, where `this` may be a subclass: it applies the declared contract of the nearest declaration of the method at or above `C`, whose table slot it loads (section 2.6), and so locks the object as well as those arguments: with `public label(): &string { return "animal"; }` in `Animal`, an override in `Dog` returning `&this.name`, and `a: &Animal`, `const tag = a.label(); a.rename("Max"); console.log(tag);` is EN0706, with `help: keep a copy: const tag = a.label().toString();` (SPEC 5.4), as Rust ties such a result to `&self`. Every other call can reach only the body itself, so it is a direct call and applies the body's own sources, `through_fields` and `through_read`, which add nothing: one whose receiver is any other place of type `C`, a local variable, a by-value parameter, a field, a temporary such as `new Animal("Rex")`, an element of an array, a fixed array or a `&Slice<C>`, a tuple part, a case field or a narrowed payload, each of which holds exactly a `C` and never a subclass, since a move never slices (SPEC 4.3), so `const t2 = pets[0].label(); pets[1].rename("Max"); console.log(t2);` compiles (`TESTING.md` `D8-receivers` and `WD2-6-element`); a `super` call; and any call of a method of a `final` class, of a `static` method, of a `private` method or of a method compiled once per type, so `this.helper(x)` to a `private helper(a: &string): &string` holds only `x` (`D8-private`). An abstract method has no base body and keeps the signature default, `this`, and `super.m()` naming an abstract `m` has no body to call, so it is EN0457 in phase 2, as `super.m()` naming a `move` method is EN0458, since that body would run on an object of the subclass (section 4). A bound may name a class (section 4), and a call of an overridable method on a place of type `T` or `&T` for a `T extends C` applies the declared contract of the method's nearest declaration at or above `C`, its base body's sources plus `this`, because the generic body is checked once and `T` may be any subclass: fitting holds every override to that contract (section 6.7), as for a call through a `&C`. The call is `Virtual` through the object's own table when `C` isn't `final`, and mono makes it a direct call in an instance for a `final` class (section 2.6). One instance is made per class passed, as for any generic, so `oldest<T extends Animal>(xs: &Slice<T>): &T` returns a `&Dog` for a `Dog[]`, and `console.log(oldest(&dogs).fetch());` prints `Fido fetches`; an override that returns `this` meets the contract, which has `this` among its sources (`TESTING.md` `D8-bound`). An overridable method's stores stay its base body's (below).

**Stores.** A contract's `stores` holds `(j, m)` for each `Param(j)`, `Fields(j)` or `RFields(j)` key in `held(caller(m))` at some `Return`; a site loan with `through_ref` there is skipped, as in the table below, because `held` already carries what it crossed. So `setSrc(s: &string) { this.src = s; }` stores `(1, 0)`, and a free function `reset(lx: &Lexer, s: &string)` that calls `lx.setSrc(s)` stores `(1, 0)` too. A store is `fields_only` when `Fields(j)` or `RFields(j)` reached `caller(m)`, `Param(j)` never did, and the class behind `j` never writes through its reference fields, as for `through_fields`: `copyFrom(o: &Lexer) { this.src = o.src; }` stores what `o`'s fields hold, so `b.copyFrom(&a)` gives `b` the loan on `a`'s source and none on `a`, and a `next` that narrows `this.src = this.src.slice(1, n)` stores `(0, 0)` without taking a loan on its own object. A store is `read_set_only` when only `RFields(j)` reached `caller(m)`, and `Param(j)` and `Fields(j)` never did, whatever the flag, as for `through_read`, and it is then never also `fields_only`: the caller stores `rfields(args[j])`, the value's read set alone, which no field writes through and which names no value, so it puts no loan on the value and counts nowhere as written through (section 6.2). So a `skip` that runs `this.src = this.src.slice(1, this.src.length());` in a `Copier` whose `step` writes through `dst` stores `(0, 0)` that way, and `c.skip(); c.step();` compiles, where storing all of `held(&c)` met the read-set check with the loan on `out` and gave `c` a loan on itself (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-skip`). A `read_set_only` store `(j, j)`, of a parameter into the value behind itself, as `skip`'s is, also runs no read-set check at the caller (section 6.2): it moves into that value, or into one its reference fields reach, only what the value's own `readonly &` fields reach, which the read-set check of the store that put it there, in whichever body made it, kept from everything the value's other fields hold and write through, and which, marked, goes into no field written through (section 6.1, rule (e)), so it puts nothing where a field writes through it. That holds at every call: a `Virtual` or bound call applies the declared store, and fitting holds every override of a method whose store `(j, j)` is `read_set_only` to a `read_set_only` store `(j, j)` or none (section 6.7), while the check on load recomputes the bit (section 6.8). The check is what failed on a value whose read set and `wfields` are both its whole set: `let c = Copier.make(&table, &out); c.skip(); c.step();` compiles, as `rustc` compiles it, where the check met `rfields(&c)`, all of `held(c)` after the call, with `wfields(c)`, the same set, EN0616 at every `c.skip()` and at `twice(&c)` (`FV-02-skip`). The store itself still applies, because the value it reaches may be one behind the value's reference fields: a `Feeder` whose `share()` runs `this.sink.setX(this.src);` through a `&View` field stores `(0, 0)` `read_set_only`, and after `f.share();` the `View` `v` that `f` was built over holds the loan on `table`, so `table.push(4);` while `v` is used is EN0706, where leaving the store out, as one that adds nothing, let `v` read `table` after the push reallocated it (`FV-02-skip`). For the same reason the EN0616s of `store` for a key of an Own `this` or a by-value borrowing parameter, below, and for a capture (section 6.4) still apply to it. Two forms stay rejected where `rustc` accepts them, named disagreements, because their sets cannot tell the value's read set from the rest: through a reference to the value that a call returned, `const r = id(&c); r.skip();`, whose read set is all it holds, the loan of `&c` included, so the store gives `c` a loan on itself and the next `c.step()` is EN0707; and in a body that reaches the value through a parameter's reference field, `w.cp.skip()` in a `Wrapper.nudge(w: &Wrapper)`, whose own store then takes `Fields(0)`, what all of `w`'s fields carry, with the read bit, so the read-set check at `Wrapper.nudge(&w)` finds the loans of `c` in `w`'s field written through, EN0616 (`FV-02-skip`). A store has the read bit when such a key reached `rfields(caller(m))`, through a store into a field declared `readonly &`, and the caller then adds what it stores to the value's read set too, and to its `wfields` only where the callee may write through the stored parameter, which is then not Read, and always for a by-value borrowing class parameter or an Own `this`, which have no permission and may bring what their own other fields write through, as `*this = u` does (`store`, section 6.2): a `Copier`'s constructor stores `(1, 0)` with the read bit for `this.src = src` and `(2, 0)` without it for `this.dst = dst`, and a `retarget(s)` running `this.src = s;` stores `(1, 0)` with it. An Own `this` has no `caller(0)` (section 6.2), so a write into it is no store: a builder `public move withSrc(s: &string): Lexer { this.src = s; this.pos = 0; return this; }` stores nothing, and its result, which holds the loan of `s` and what the consumed object's fields held, stays writable. Nor can a store reach a value through an Own `this`, or through a by-value borrowing parameter, which has no `caller(m)` either: nothing would carry it to the caller's value, so a call that stores into a value reached through such a parameter's keys, as `this.cp.retarget(s)` does in a `move` method of a `Wrapper` with a `&Copier` field, is EN0616, `` cannot store a reference into a value reached through consumed `this` ``, or `` reached through by-value `w` `` for `w.cp.retarget(s)`, with `help: write the field directly, or take the value by reference` (`store`, section 6.2). The rule fires on a call that stores into any value whose set holds a key of such a parameter, because the keys cannot tell the value itself from what its fields reach, nor a value built from its fields or tokens from one reached through them, since a join or a call result may mix them: `this.setSrc(s)` in a `move` method and `w.setSrc(s)` on a by-value `let w` are EN0616 too, and so are `c.retarget(s)` after `let c = new Copier(s, w.dst);` in a `rebuild(w: Copier, s: &u8[])`, where `c` holds `Param(w)` through the copy of `w.dst`, and `this.lx.setSrc(s)` after `this.lx = lx;` in a constructor taking a by-value `lx: Lexer`, where `this` then holds `Param(lx)`. Where the stored reference outlives the value, `rustc` accepts each of these, a named disagreement, while a direct write such as `this.src = s` stores nothing and compiles (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-lost-store`, `BC-07`). A reference parameter is no such parameter: the caller's own loans carry its stores back, so `let lx = new Lexer(s); lx.setSrc(t);` in a `relen(s: &string, t: &string)` compiles (`FV-02-lost-store`). A body containing `unsafe` infers its stores from its safe operations like any other body; only its sources fall back to the signature defaults. A position with no body declares no stores, and an overridable method declares its base body's, which fitting enforces, bits included (section 6.7).

**Definition check at each `Return`.** For each key in `held(_0)`:

| Key | Result |
|---|---|
| `Param(i)`, `Fields(i)` or `RFields(i)`, `i` among the sources, except `Param(0)` of an Own `this` | ok |
| `Param(0)` of an Own `this`, in a `move` method | EN0710, `` `posRef` returns a reference into the object it consumes ``: the object is disposed when the method ends |
| `Param(i)`, `Fields(i)` or `RFields(i)`, `i` outside sources fixed elsewhere: a signature default, a base body's sources plus `this`, or a declared set; or `Param(i)` where the base body puts `i`, other than `this`, in `through_fields`, or `Param(i)` or `Fields(i)` where it puts `i` in `through_read` | EN0714 |
| `Site(L)` with `through_ref` | Ignored: `held` already carries what the reference it crossed holds |
| `Site(L)` on a locally owned path, including by-value parameters | EN0710 |
| `Site(L)` on a `Temp` | EN0712 |

A local function's hidden arguments are never among its sources, so returning a reference derived from one is EN0714 too.

`StorageDead(s)` and `Drop(s)` run before `Return`, so a loan held by `_0` would first surface as EN0711. The checker maps a conflict whose only live holder is `_0` to EN0710, with the borrow site as primary span when the borrow lies in the `return` that fills `_0`, and otherwise with that `return` as primary span and the borrow as secondary, and dedupes per loan.

**Call check.** The `Call` transfer above applies the callee's contract: its sources, `through_fields` and `through_read`, its stores with their `fields_only`, `read_set_only` and read bits, and through N8 each argument's mode, part by part for a by-value borrowing tuple. Checking is modular over contracts, not over signatures: an edit to a body alone can change its contract, and so what its callers may do, which is why `sigHash` includes the contract (`DEV_RUNTIME.md` section 5).

### 6.4 Closures

The arrow body is checked as its own `Body`, with `_env` a reference parameter. Moving out of `*(_env.i)` is EN0617 by capture origin. A captured reference local cannot be rebound inside the arrow (EN0616), so a closure cannot stash a reference argument outside; writes through it are ordinary exclusive captures. For the same reason the arrow cannot store into a borrowing value reached through `_env`: a call whose stores target a value derived from a capture, as `words.forEach(w => { lx.setSrc(w); })` does, and a reference-field write through a captured `this` or reference, are EN0616 (`` cannot store a reference into captured `lx` ``), since a call through a closure value applies no stores and `_env` has no `caller(m)` to carry them (`store`, section 6.2). That also covers `(*(*_env).0).src = u`, which would cross two references. The result type is `V`, so nothing leaves. At the creation site, `held(env)` keeps every capture loan alive for as long as the closure value is live: until the call returns for an arrow argument, and until its last use for an arrow stored in a local, so `inc(); console.log(count); inc();` with `const inc = () => { count += 1; };` is EN0715.

**Why a writing capture through a reference makes a loan.** Copying the local would put no loan on `*r`, and a later reborrow of `*r` in another argument would see no conflict. With the site loan, `both(() => { *r += 1; }, r)` is EN0715 in either argument order. The capture also holds a shared loan on the local `r` itself (section 5), so `const f = () => console.log(r); r = &y; f();` is EN0715, `` `r` is locked while `f` will still be used ``, where a copy alone would print the old target while a local function prints the new one.

**Transfer for closure parameters.** A closure value is an exclusive reborrow, so `f` behaves as an always-exclusive reference local that can only be called, captured or forwarded, and, when it is nullable (`handler?: (k: u8) => void`), tested against `null`, which reads only its tag. Each forward takes a loan on `f` for the callee, so `twice(f, f)` conflicts and `visit(f, n); visit(f, m);` does not.

### 6.5 Data for diagnostics

```rust
pub struct BorrowConflict {
    pub code: Code, pub access: Access,          // place, kind, span, method name if via a receiver
    pub loan: LoanId, pub holder: Option<Local>,
    pub later_use: Option<(Local, Span)>,        // the "still in use here" label
    pub at: Point,
}
```

- **`later_use`.** BFS forward from P over the CFG to the first statement using any slot that holds the loan there, preferring user-named slots over hidden ones. The use may be a hidden head read: in `const row = &grid[0]; for (const c of row) { grid.push([]); }` the push conflicts with the loan of `&grid[0]` that `row` holds, not with the loop's hold, which covers only `row` and `*row`, and the only later use of `row` is the head, labelled `the loop reads `row` again here`. A loop's hold has no later use to show: its secondary label is the source.
- **Loan origin selects wording.** A loop's `Hold`, and `ForOfHidden` for `Once` places, give the owner's `for...of` wording (SPEC 14 G08 and G21): one message for a structural change of the source and one for its reassignment, each labelling the source and the change. `ForOfElement` names the loop variable; `AutoBorrow` names the method; `ClosureCapture` names the arrow; `HiddenArg` names the local function and its variable; `CompoundTarget` names the assignment's target and gives the help that splits the statement (section 5). A conflict with a loan that a view or a `slice` result holds on an array labels the change `` `list` grows here `` only when it grows the array, and `` `list` changes here `` for any other change (SPEC 8.3).
- **Fix-its** live in `explain.rs`, keyed by code, and each must compile after application (`{ fixes: true }` tests, which accept warnings in the fixed program). The `[...q]` edit of G08 and the `q.clone()` edit for a reassigned source change `x` from `&T` to `T`, so they are offered only when the hold is shared, which means the body never writes through `x`, when `T` satisfies `is_clone`, and when the body has no explicit deref of `x`. The help in G07 that suggests borrowing `u` instead is offered when a bit stored with the callee's contract says its body neither moves nor writes that parameter (sections 6.7 and 6.8), when the callee is in user code.

### 6.6 Worked traces

**G11** (`r = &s` in an inner block): `held(r) = {L(s)}` after line 5. `StorageDead(s)` at line 6 is checked; `r` is live because line 7 uses it. `L(s)` is live and shallow does not apply, because `s` is owned. EN0711, with `later_use = line 7`.

**NLL problem case #3** (`getOrInsert`, SPEC 5.3): `hit` holds `{L(*m...), Param(m)}` in the branch that returns it. On the other path `hit` is dead, so the loan is not live and `m.insert(k)` is accepted. There are no regions, so nothing can extend the loan to the other branch.

**G21** (`q = []` inside `for (const x of &q)`, after the last use of `x`): the phantom slot holds `Hold` on `q` from the pre-header, and the head is reachable from `q = []`, so the hold is live at the store. D3's `Drop` has no check of its own, and the store writes `q` against a live shared loan: EN0706, with the reassignment wording and the source as secondary. With `q = []; break;` the head is not reachable from the store, the hold is dead there, and the loop compiles.

**G08** (`queue.push("retry")` inside `for (const job of &queue)`): the hold's shared loan on `queue` is live at the push, because the head is reachable from it. `push` writes its receiver, so N4 makes `_t = &queue [auto-borrow]` exclusive, and an exclusive borrow against a live shared loan is EN0706, with the structural-change wording. The hold is tried before `job`'s element loan, so the report is the same whether or not `job` is used after the push.

**Tokens of one `Lexer`** (SPEC 5.1: `let lx = new Lexer(&source); const a = lx.next(); const b = lx.next();`): no body of `Lexer` writes through `src`, so its flag is off, and the constructor's contract stores `(1, 0)` with `src` Read: `held(lx) = {L(source)}`, a shared loan. Each `lx.next()` takes `_r = &lx [auto-borrow]`, exclusive because `next` writes `this.pos`, with `held(_r) = {L(lx)} ∪ held(lx)` and `fields(_r) = held(lx)`. `lx` is a variable of class `Lexer`, which holds exactly a `Lexer`, so the call applies `next`'s own contract (section 6.3), and `next` has `this` in `through_fields`, because the flag is off and its token, a string part, is read-only, so `held(a) = fields(_r) = {L(source)}`. Nothing holds `L(lx)` after the call, so the second auto-borrow is accepted while `a` is live, and `source = "x";` followed by a use of `a` is EN0706, after `lx`'s last use too, because `a` holds `L(source)`. A method returning `&this.pos` instead holds `Param(0)`, so its result keeps `L(lx)` and a second call while it is live is EN0707. Unless `Lexer` is `final`, so is a second `r.next()` through an `r: &Lexer` while the first token is live: that call is `Virtual`, and its declared sources keep `this` out of `through_fields`, since an override may return into the object.

**A class that writes through its field** (`Holder` with `private v: &i64[]`, a constructor storing `v`, `public first(): &i64 { return &this.v[0]; }` and `public reset(): void { *this.v = [9, 9]; }`): `reset` writes through `this.v`, so the flag is on. The constructor's `this.v = v` is then a store into a value whose class writes through its fields, so `v` is write-live by rule (e) and Write, and `let h = new Holder(&data);` takes an exclusive loan on `data` that `h` holds: `const q = &data[0];` while `h` is still used is EN0708. `first` reaches `this` only through `Fields(0)`, but with the flag on `this` is an ordinary source, so `const e = h.first();` holds `{L(h), L(data)}`, and `h.reset()` while `e` is live is EN0706. Without the flag, `e` would hold `L(data)` alone, and `reset` would free the buffer `e` points into.

**A token from a `readonly &` field** (`Copier` with `private src: readonly &Slice<u8>`, `private dst: &Slice<u8>` and `private pos: isize`, a `step()` that runs `this.dst[this.pos] = this.src[this.pos]; this.pos += 1;`, and `public peek(): readonly &Slice<u8> { return this.src.slice(this.pos, this.pos + 1); }`): `step` writes through `dst`, so the flag is on. The constructor stores `(2, 0)` for `this.dst = dst`, a store by rule (e), so `dst` is Write, and `(1, 0)` with the read bit for `this.src = src`, which rule (e) skips, so `src` is Read. Over `const table: u8[3] = [7, 8, 9];` and `let out: u8[3] = [0, 0, 0];`, `let c = new Copier(&table, &out);` gives `held(c) = {L(table), L(out)}`, `L(table)` shared and `L(out)` exclusive, and `rfields(c) = {L(table)}`. `peek` reads `this.src` through a `readonly &` field, so its result holds `RFields(0)` and neither `Fields(0)` nor `Param(0)`, and `this` is in `through_read`: `const a = c.peek();` gives `held(a) = rfields(_r) = {L(table)}`. `c.step()` borrows `c` exclusively, which overlaps no loan that `a` holds, so `const a = c.peek(); c.step(); c.step(); const b = c.peek(); console.log(a, b);` compiles and prints `[7] [9]`, and `console.log(table, out);` then prints `[7, 8, 9] [7, 8, 0]`. Moving or dropping `c` leaves `a` valid for the same reason. A `last()` returning `this.dst.slice(0, 1)` holds `Fields(0)`, which with the flag on keeps `this` an ordinary source, so its result holds `L(c)`, and `c.step()` while it is live is EN0706, as for `Holder` (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-copier`).

**Complexity.** Slots times loans bitsets per block; functions have few of each. Target under 1 ms for a typical function, and a 1,000-function file in under 300 ms in release [target; rustc checked the same volume in 0.29 s measured].

### 6.7 Contracts

`contract(def)` (section 3) computes what a caller sees of a function (SPEC 5): for each `&` parameter and `this`, Read or Write, and for `this` also Own, and Read or Write for each reference part of a by-value borrowing tuple parameter (below); the result's sources, and which of them it reaches only through a borrowing value's reference fields, or only through its fields declared `readonly &` (section 6.3); whether a returned reference is none, read-only with its first reason, or writable; and which parameters it stores in a borrowing value behind another parameter, in `this` for a method of a borrowing class or behind any parameter whose pointee is a borrowing value, each store with its `fields_only`, `read_set_only` and read bits (section 6.3); and, for each by-value parameter, whether the body moves or writes it, which only G07's help reads (section 6.5), so it is no part of `sigHash` (section 6.8). Each borrowing class and borrowing tuple type also gets one flag, whether it writes through its reference fields (below). Type checking never reads a contract (N1). After phase 2 they are computed per SCC, on demand, so unreached library bodies stay untyped. The graph has a node per function and per borrowing class: a function has an edge to each function it calls and to each class whose flag it reads, by rule (e), for its own `through_fields` and `fields_only` stores or for the read-set check of a store (section 6.2), and a class has an edge to each body that sees its `private` members: its methods, `static` ones included, its constructors, parameter properties included, its destructor, and every arrow nested in any of these, at any depth, which belongs to the body it is written in, and to its base class, whose flag it includes (below). Nothing else sees its `private` reference fields, a test item of its module included (section 4), so the flag is the same in every `ParseMode`, and `build` and `check` infer the same contracts, layouts and API hash for a module (`TESTING.md` `S2-26-test`).

**Writes through reference fields.** A borrowing class's flag is set when, in any of its bodies, the only code that sees its `private` reference fields, that is its methods, `static` ones included, its constructors, parameter properties included, its destructor and every arrow nested in them, a reference read from one of its reference fields not declared `readonly &` is write-live where it is read (N3, below): written through, passed to a Write parameter, or copied into a slot that is. It is also set when such a body returns that reference, or a part of it, as the returned reference or as a reference part of a returned tuple, in a result that may be writable: one with no `readonly` before its return type and no read-only mark on the returned value (section 6.1), so a token that is a string part, as `Lexer.next` returns, sets nothing. A constructor counts as a method does, since it sees the fields too: a `Grower` whose constructor pushes through the `v` it stores has its flag set, so `new Grower(&data)` while a reference into `data` is still used is EN0706 at `&data`, as rustc's E0502 is, and over a `const data` it is EN0608; an arrow that pushes through `this.v` inside a method `grow` sets it as well, so `g.grow()` while a token `t` taken from `g` is live is EN0706. A returned borrowing class value does not count, since writes through it go through its own class's methods, which that class's flag and rule (e) govern. A class's flag is also set when its base class's flag is set: a base's bodies write through the base's fields of a subclass value as of any other, and every rule that reads the flag of the class behind a value, `through_fields`, `fields_only`, rule (e), the read-set check and EN0418, must see those writes, where a `Sub` whose own bodies wrote through nothing let `Base.firstOf(o: &Sub)`, returning `&o.dst[0]`, hand out a token that `s.step()` then freed. The base's flag is in its module's hashed API section, which the subclass's module imports, so the subclass's flag moves with it and G3 holds (section 6.8, `TESTING.md` `FV-02-base-flag`, `S2-08-copy`). Writing the field itself, as `this.src = this.src.slice(1, n)` does, sets nothing, since repointing never writes the old pointee. A borrowing tuple has no methods to infer from, so a reference part reached behind a reference always counts as written, and its flag is always set. A field whose type is, or contains outside any borrowing-class field, a borrowing tuple counts as a reference field (section 4), and since `this` puts the tuple's parts behind a reference, it sets its class's flag, as the tuple type's own flag is set, unless every reference part of it, at every depth, a nullable payload included, is declared `readonly`: a reference that reaches such a field, `&this.t`, is read-only as a whole (section 6.1), so nothing writes through a part, and with `private t: [readonly &i64[], i32]` the flag stays off, the constructor's parameter is Read and `new Pad(&k)` over a `const k` compiles, while `p.tup()[1] = 5;` is EN0616 naming `t` (`TESTING.md` `FV-05-pad`). One plain reference part, nested or not, still sets it, as in `private t: [readonly &i64[], [&i64[], i32]]` (`TESTING.md` `FV-05-nested`): with `private t: [&i64[], i32]` stored from the constructor, `public first(): readonly &i64 { return &this.t[0][0]; }` keeps `this` an ordinary source, so with `public tup(): &[&i64[], i32] { return &this.t; }`, `p.tup()[0].push(4);` while a token from `p.first()` is live is EN0706. One flag per class follows SPEC 5.1's main loan set per borrowing value; its read set needs none, since nothing writes through it (section 6.2). The flag goes into the interface file's hashed section (section 6.8), and it decides three things: rule (e) makes a reference stored into such a value write-live, so its loan is exclusive, as Rust's `&'a mut` field is, unless the field it is stored into is declared `readonly &`, as Rust's `&'a T` field is; `through_fields` and `fields_only` stores apply only when it is off, while `through_read` applies whatever it is (section 6.3); and a class whose flag is set cannot be Copy, since a copy would duplicate its exclusive stored loans, which a class copies as they are (section 6.2), so `implements Copy` on it is EN0418 in phase 3, `` `Cursor` writes through its reference fields, so copying it would make two writers ``, with `` help: if `Cursor` only reads through them, write `readonly` before their types or before the return types that hand them out, or remove `implements Copy` `` (SPEC 4.3). Typing still treats the class as Copy (N1); only that verdict waits for the flag. Wherever the flag's witness (below) is a writable result rather than a write, EN0608 at a store that rule (e) rejects, and EN0706 or EN0707 where the loss of `through_fields` keeps an earlier result's loan on the object, name the field and add `` help: if `ByteLexer` only reads `src`, declare `private src: readonly &Slice<u8>;` ``, then the method-level form, `` help: or, if callers only read what `next` returns, write `public next(): readonly &Slice<u8> | null` ``. A reference field declared `readonly &` (section 4) never sets the flag: a reference read from it carries a read-only mark (section 6.1), so writing through it or passing it to a Write parameter anywhere in its class is EN0616 at the write, naming the field, and a result reached from it is read-only (stage 4), as a result is as changeable as what it comes from. So with `private src: &Slice<u8>`, a `ByteLexer` whose `next` returns `this.src.slice(start, this.pos)` with no `readonly` has its flag set, `new ByteLexer(&bytes)` over a `const` buffer is EN0608 and a second `ly.next()` while the first token is used is EN0707, each with those helps; with `private src: readonly &Slice<u8>;` the flag is off, the constructor's `src` is Read, `next`'s result is read-only and has `this` in `through_read` (section 6.3), and both compile, as they do with `readonly` before `next`'s return type alone (`TESTING.md` `S2-27-bytes`). Every other reference field keeps the writable default, and the flag's other witnesses are unchanged. In a class whose flag another field sets, a `readonly &` field still takes its references shared, since rule (e) skips a store into it, so a `Copier` with `private src: readonly &Slice<u8>;` and a `dst` it writes through is built over a `const` source, `new Copier(&data, &buf)`, while the flag still keeps the class from being Copy (EN0418). `through_fields` still needs the flag off, but a token read from `src` alone is `through_read` (section 6.3): it holds the loans of the value's read set and none on the object, so `const a = c.peek(); c.step(); console.log(a);` with a `step` that writes through `dst` compiles, as Rust's `fn peek(&self) -> &'a [u8]` does, while a token read from `dst` holds the object, so `const d = c.last(); c.step(); console.log(d);` with `public last(): readonly &Slice<u8> { return this.dst.slice(0, 1); }` is EN0706 (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-copier`). For that, a `readonly &` field holds only loans that nothing in the value writes through: in a class whose flag is set, storing into it a reference read through another reference field of the same value, as `this.src = this.dst.slice(0, 1);` does, is EN0616 (section 6.2), while a reference from a parameter or from another `readonly &` field may be stored. A destructor may read or write through the fields (SPEC 5.8 I5), so the drop of a value it runs on is a use of the value's loans (section 6.2), exclusive ones when the flag is on.

**Stages inside an SCC (N7).** Each stage has one answer, so visit order cannot matter.

1. **Own.** `this` is Own iff the method is marked `move`. In an unmarked method, or in a constructor, moving `this`, or a non-`Copy` field out of `this`, is EN0603 at the method.
2. **Sources and stores,** a least fixed point, every function of the SCC starting with empty sets. It tracks three sets of parameters, those whose `Param(i)` key reaches `held(_0)` at some `Return`, those whose `Fields(i)` key does and those whose `RFields(i)` key does, and for each `m` the keys that reach `held(caller(m))` and those that reach `rfields(caller(m))`; a call to a function of the SCC applies that function's current sets through the `Call` transfer, a `Virtual` call its base body's with `this` added (section 6.3). The sources are the parameters any of these keys reaches, `through_fields` is the `Fields` set minus the `Param` set, kept only for a parameter whose class has its flag off, `through_read` is the `RFields` set minus the `Param` and `Fields` sets, whatever the flag, and the stores and their `fields_only`, `read_set_only` and read bits follow section 6.3, a `read_set_only` store `(j, j)` included, whose read-set check the caller skips. Whether a key is held depends on no mode or permission, only on these sets and on the flags of stage 3, and both only grow.
3. **Permissions and flags,** a least fixed point over Read < Write and over off < on: a parameter is Write iff it is write-live at entry, where rule (d) reads the sources of stage 2 and rule (e) the flags, and a flag is set by the rules above, which read these permissions and results. Where a flag rule asks whether a result may be writable, a call to a function of the same SCC counts as writable unless its return type says `readonly`, which is where stage 4 starts, so a flag is only ever set too often, never too rarely. A `let` reference parameter is Write even if the body only reads, and `let` before a method's name makes `this` Write the same way: an overridable method whose own body leaves the object alone writes it when overrides may change the object, as `public let update(): void {}` does. In a body containing `unsafe` or `asm` (nested arrows included), an `unsafe function` or an `@naked` function, which the MIR's recorded regions show (section 2.5), every reference and `this` starts as Write, and `const` before one lowers it to Read (N12). When such a body is one of a borrowing class's bodies (a method, `static` or not, a constructor, the destructor, or an arrow nested in one of them), a reference it reads from one of the class's reference fields likewise counts as write-live where it is read, and so sets the flag, unless the method is marked `const`, or the field is declared `readonly &` or the tuple part it is read from is declared `readonly`, a promise such a body keeps as it keeps a `const` mark (SPEC 5.9), since a raw-pointer write through the field's target is invisible to N3: a `put` that writes into `this.buf` through a raw pointer makes the loan its constructor stored exclusive. The safe operations of such a body are then checked as if that reference were shared, transitively, so a `const` body that forwards to a default-Write helper is EN0615. Stage 2 reads the flags this stage sets, so in an SCC that holds a class, stages 2 and 3 repeat together until neither changes. Every fact in them only grows, and a set flag only makes a call hold more, so their joint answer is the least one.
4. **Result,** a greatest fixed point: start writable and fall by N6. It is read-only, with the first reason, when `readonly` stands before the return type; when it is a reference, a nullable one or a tuple with a reference part at any depth, and has a source in `through_read` (stage 2), whatever marks the body's flows carry, `` `find` returns a reference reached from the `readonly &` field `rows` ``, so no read-set loan ever reaches a writable reference and the read set's soundness never rests on how complete the marks are (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-finder`), while a borrowing class value with such a source stays writable, since a reference taken from a `readonly &` field is marked and rule (e) keeps it out of any class that writes through its fields (`BC-08-readonly`); when any returned value may carry a read-only mark (section 6.1), so a result that may point at a constant or static stays read-only; or when it is a `&string` or `&string | null`, or a tuple with a `&string` part, that may come from a Read `&string` or `&string | null` parameter, or from a Read `&string` part of a by-value tuple parameter, which may be a part of a string, as a `slice` result is, so it has the two-word form and can't grow (section 7.7): `wrap(s: &string): [&string, i32] { return [s, 1]; }` returns a read-only tuple, and `const w = wrap(&t); *w[0] += "!";` is EN0616. A Read `&T` or `&T | null` parameter, or a Read `&T` part of a by-value tuple parameter, is not tested, since a generic body's contract never depends on `T` (SPEC 4.10); its instance at `T := string` takes the whole form instead (section 7.7). A result derived from `this` is as writable as one derived from any other parameter, as in TypeScript: `public book(id: i64): &Book { return &this.books[id - 1]; }` serves reading and writing, and `public count(): readonly &isize { return &this.n; }` protects what it hands out. It also falls when a source `j` is stored as well, with `(j, m)` among the stores of stage 2 (`` `keep` also keeps a reference from `x` in `this` ``), because the stored copy survives the return beside the result: the read-only result keeps the caller from writing through it, and the stored copy and the result never both point at `j`'s data while one of them may write, because a store into a class that writes through its reference fields holds its exclusive borrow until the callee returns, so no path of the body both stores `j` and returns from it (section 6.2), unless the field is declared `readonly &`, whose stored copy is shared and never written through, so both holders only read. A result with more than one reference part, a borrowing tuple, a nullable one included, falls when two of its parts would conflict as copies into write-live slots (O7) (`` `two` returns two references that may point at the same data ``). The parts are every reference part of the returned value, nested tuple parts and the payload of a nullable included, and a borrowing class value, returned on its own or as a part, counts as one part holding its whole set: writes through its fields happen only in its own class's bodies, where borrows through two of its fields stay apart only because two fields that point at the same data conflict where that aliasing is made (section 6.2, **Through a borrowing value**) and copies out of them take loans (section 5), its stores are guarded by rule (e), which makes a stored loan on a field written through exclusive, so aliasing arguments conflict at the store, and by the read-set check, and a token from it holds the object unless it is `through_fields`, with nothing written through, or `through_read`, so a factory such as `make(s, d): Copier { return new Copier(s, d); }`, or one that writes a field of the value after building it, returns a writable value (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-alias-other`, `FV-02-make`). A part that an `Aggregate` evaluated in the returning statement placed, looking through `Aggregate(present)` and nested tuple `Aggregate`s, is compared by that operand's loans, so `return [[r, r], 0]` at `[[&i64[], &i64[]], i32] | null` is read-only (`TK-01-nested`). A part of a local whose only reaching definition is such an `Aggregate`, provided nothing writes the local or any part of it, or borrows it exclusively, before the return, is compared with the other parts of that local by the loans, and keys with their bits, that the `Aggregate`'s operands held at that definition, since two references that were disjoint when the literal was built stay disjoint while nothing writes the local, so `const t = [&p.a, &p.b]; return t;` stays writable (`TESTING.md` `TK-01-local`); and it is compared with every other part of the returned value by what the local holds at the return, `held(t)` after every rename, mark change and kill since the definition (O9), because those loans may have changed meaning: a literal built before an inner loop's pre-header renamed its loans of that loop's site to `EarlierRuns`, returned beside that loop's binding, and one built before a `let` parameter was repointed, returned beside a borrow through its new target, are read-only (`TK-01-local-stale`, `TK-01-local-kill`), where the operands' loans at the definition made both writable pairs of one element. A call result, in the returning statement, or in a local whose only reaching definition is that call, provided nothing writes the local or any part of it, or borrows it exclusively, before the return, counts as one part only when its arguments for the callee's sources pass the part test against each other, by the sets they held at the call, each reference part of a by-value tuple argument counting as an argument of its own, compared with the other parts of that argument by the loans and keys its operand held when the argument is an `Aggregate`, or a local or temporary whose only reaching definition is one that nothing wrote or borrowed exclusively before the call, and otherwise by the argument's whole set, and with every other argument by what the argument holds at the call, `held(t)` after every rename, mark change and kill since the definition, so a tuple local built before a `let` parameter was repointed meets a borrow through its new target as the whole parameter (`TK-01-local-kill`, H40); parts are compared through traces, the position and set at each level of the nested literals and locals they come through, meeting at the first level where their positions differ, a `through_fields` source by what its fields hold and a `through_read` source by its read set; otherwise each of its parts holds the call's whole set; one kept in such a local is compared with every other part by what the local holds at the return, as above, and one kept in a local that is written or borrowed exclusively after the call holds the local's whole set, as below. So `dup` returning `two(r, r)` is read-only, and `b.push(9)` through its result is EN0614, a changing method called through a read-only reference, while a direct write such as `clash(p[0], p[1])` is EN0616 (`TK-01-dup`), `let p = two(&q.xs, &q.ys); p[0] = p[1]; return p;` is read-only, since `p` is written after the call, so both parts hold `held(p)`, whose loan on `q.xs` meets itself, where looking through the write returned `[&q.ys, &q.ys]` writable (`TK-01-split-written`), `return two(a, x)` with `a` kept from an earlier turn of the same run stays writable (`TK-01-two-turns`), as `halves(v)` returning `v.splitAt(2)` does (`TK-01-split-wrap`), and `mk(x)` returning `id2([x, x])` is read-only (`TK-01-tuple-arg`). A by-value tuple parameter that the body never writes, in whole or in part, and never borrows exclusively counts as one part, since at a call site N8's per-part loans keep its parts disjoint where the result is write-live, and this rule for call results does where the caller only returns it, so `id2` alone returns a writable tuple. Every other reference part, a part of a local or temporary tuple that was written after it was built included, holds that value's whole loan set (O9), so any two such parts conflict, unless the set is empty. Each part is compared through its compared loans: every site loan it holds, bound by a loop or not, and, for each `Param(i)`, `Fields(i)` or `RFields(i)` key it holds bare (section 6.2, covered keys), the whole parameter, the path `(i, through_ref)` for `Param(i)` and `(i, through_ref, [Fields])` for `Fields(i)` and `RFields(i)`; a covered key is left to the site loans that cross it, which the part holds (a loan on an owned field, `&this.own`, crosses `Param(0)` but not `Fields(0)`). A flat set loses where a key came from, so a crossing loan in the set does not show that the key came with it: with `const s = p;`, which takes no loan, `const second = c ? &p.ys : &s.xs; return [&p.xs, second];` holds `Param(p)` bare from `s` beside the loan on `p.ys` that crosses it, so the key is compared as the whole of `p`, which overlaps `&p.xs`, and the pair is read-only, where letting the crossing loan stand for it made both parts `p.xs` (`TESTING.md` `TK-01-copy-join`); and `const p = c ? &this.own : this.view; return [p, &this.view[0]];` in a `Holder` whose flag is on holds the loan that copying `this.view` takes (section 5), which overlaps `&this.view[0]`, and stays read-only (`TK-01-pick`). Two parts conflict when some compared loan of one and some compared loan of the other overlap by `loans_overlap`, taken pair by pair, with one exception: a pair formed by one key K that both parts hold, each only via bind l (section 6.2), a site loan, a loan of another loop's site included, as an inner loop over the outer loop's binding marks the outer loop's loan of this turn, or a parameter's key compared as the whole parameter, but never a loan of l's own site, which l's bind never marks, is not compared by path; it is disjoint when each part holds a loan of l's bind site, since those site loans are compared as pairs themselves (this turn's key against the key of an earlier turn of the same run is disjoint, any other pair overlaps), and it overlaps when either part holds none. Every other pair is compared by path, a key one part holds plainly, or via another bind, against a key the other part holds via l included, so nothing is ever left out of the comparison. So parts from disjoint fields of one parameter, or from different parameters, stay writable, while parts reached through one local reference, or through one turn's loop binding, share that slot's loans and conflict even when their fields differ: `const r = &p.inner; return [&r.a, &r.b];`, and `[&p.a, &p.b]` for a binding `p` of a loop, are read-only where `rustc` accepts both (`TESTING.md` `TK-01-local-ref`, `TK-03-fields-of-turn`), two named disagreements (`TESTING.md` section 13), while in a borrowing class `[&this.a, &this.b]` over two owned fields stays writable, and so does `[&this.own, &this.view[0]]`, since a reference taken through `this` to a place whose type holds no reference takes no `Fields(0)` or `RFields(0)` key (section 6.2), so each part is compared by its own loan (`TK-01-owned-fields`, `TK-01-own-view`). Two loans of one `for...of` bind site that the exception does not decide are compared by `loans_overlap` (section 6.2), which finds this turn's key and the key of an earlier turn of the same run disjoint, so `pairBig(xs: &Slice<i64>): [&i64, &i64] | null`, which keeps `a = x` from one turn and returns `[a, x]` from a later turn of the same run, returns a writable pair, and writing `0` through both parts of `pairBig(&q)` over `q = [1, 5, 3, 7]` leaves `[1, 0, 0, 7]`, as Rust allows. The pair stays writable over a view or a copy of the parameter, since both parts then hold `xs`'s key, and the receiver loan of `xs.slice(1)`, only via the bind: `pairBig` with `for (const x of xs.slice(1))` writes `[9, 1, 0, 0, 7]` over `[9, 1, 5, 3, 7]`, and one with `const r = xs; for (const x of r)` writes `[1, 0, 0, 7]` (`TESTING.md` `TK-03-tail`, `TK-03-copied-ref`), while a part `a = &xs[1]`, which holds `xs`'s key plainly, keeps the pair read-only (`TK-03-tail-mixed`), and so does a part that took `x` only on some paths, `if (c) { a = x; }` after `a = &xs[2]` (`TK-03-tail-mixed-join`), or that joins bindings of two loops (`TK-03-two-loops`). A pair from two turns of an inner loop over one turn's binding of an outer loop, `for (const x of rows) { for (const y of x) { ... return [a, y]; } }`, is writable too: both parts hold the outer loop's loan of this turn and `rows`'s key only via the inner bind, and inner-site loans of two turns of one run, so `innerPair(&grid)` writes `[[0, 0, 3], [4]]` over `[[1, 2, 3], [4]]`, as Rust allows (`TESTING.md` `TK-03-inner-pair`). `[r, r]` from one turn, whose parts both carry this turn's key, is read-only, and so is a pair kept from two runs of a loop inside a `while`, whose first part carries the key of earlier runs, so `clash(p0, p1)` on it is EN0616 at `p1` (`TESTING.md` `S2-25-pair`). A pair formed after the loop, as `firstTwo` returns, or the last two matches kept across turns, as `lastTwo` returns, stays read-only too, since telling its parts apart would need a path-by-path test after the loop or the order of earlier turns, and writing through it is EN0616 with `` help: return the pair inside the loop `` (`TK-03-first-two`, `TK-03-last-two`), two named disagreements with `rustc` (`TESTING.md` section 13). Otherwise a result derived from parameters, `this` included, is writable, and the caller's use decides (rule (d) below).
5. **Check** each body once, against the contracts just computed.

Own joins above Write. Inside a method `this` has type `&C`; at an expected type `C`, a non-`Copy` `this` is a move, legal only in a `move` method; `const me = this` is a reference copy; `*this = v` writes the whole value; `this` is never rebound.

**Write-liveness (N3).** `wlive(s, point)` is a backward may-analysis on MIR: true if some path from the point, not reassigning `s` first, reaches (a) a write through `s`: a whole value, a field, an element, a compound assignment or `++`; (b) `s` as argument or receiver of a Write parameter, of a function, a bound member, an `fn` value or a closure parameter; (c) a copy, projection, destructuring binding, narrowing, ternary, `??`, `!`, `?.`, reference conversion (`Convert`: C18, C21, C22), capture or `for...of` bind into a slot that is write-live just after, and, for a reference part of a borrowing tuple held directly (a local, a parameter or a temporary), a nullable one included, a borrow `&d` of that tuple into a slot that is write-live just after, which makes every reference part of `d`, nested parts included, write-live at that point; (d) `s` as argument of a parameter among the callee's sources, where the call's result is write-live just after; (e) `s` stored into a borrowing value whose class writes through its reference fields, or into a borrowing tuple reached through a reference: `(*t).f = s` or `d.f = s` for a reference or borrowing field `f`, at any depth, or `*t = s` for a borrowing pointee, where a class's own tuple field counts as part of its class, not as a tuple reached through a reference, so a store into it, or into a part of it at any depth, is write-live exactly when the class's flag is set, as `TESTING.md` `FV-05-pad` relies on; a call that stores `s` into such a value is covered by (b), since the callee's own parameter is Write by this rule. A store whose target is a reference field declared `readonly &` is skipped, whether it is `(*t).f = s`, `d.f = s`, a parameter property or another constructor store into that `f`, since nothing writes through that field (section 6.1), while a whole-value write `*t = s` keeps (e). `return s` is not a write. A borrowing class value, nullable or not, held in a local, a temporary or a by-value parameter or returned by a call, is never write-live itself: neither a write of one of its fields, nor its use as a receiver, a `move` receiver included, nor its use as an argument makes it so, since writes through its reference fields happen only in its class's bodies, where rule (e) gave each stored loan the mode of the parameter that filled the field; so rule (d) and N8 ask whether a result is write-live only of a reference result and of the reference parts of a tuple result, and a factory's source takes its mode from its permission: `let c = Copier.make(&table, &out); c.step();` over a `const table` compiles (`TESTING.md` `FV-02-class-writelive`). Rule (d) is how one accessor serves reading and writing: `players.last()` borrows `players` for writing exactly when a later line writes through the result. Rule (e) is how a field's write is inferred from the class's own bodies: `g.setV(&data)` borrows `data` for writing when some method, constructor or destructor of `g`'s class, or an arrow nested in one, pushes through the stored field, and a `const` or literal source is then rejected at the store (section 6.1).

**Tuple parts.** A by-value parameter of a borrowing tuple type has a permission for each reference part, nested tuple parts included, inferred as for a `&` parameter: part `p.k` is Write iff it is write-live at entry, with N3 treating `p.k` as a slot of its own, written through `*p.k` or copied into a write-live slot. N3 tracks any borrowing tuple slot, a nullable one included, part by part until a reference to it is taken: (b) makes part `k` of an argument write-live when the parameter's part `k` is Write, and (c) carries that back to the operand an `Aggregate` placed in that part, so in `grow([&s, 1])` with a `grow` that pushes through `t[0]`, N8 makes the loan of `&s` exclusive and N5 checks its root. A write-live `&d` puts the parts behind a reference, where nothing tells them apart, as a borrowing tuple's flag already assumes, so (c) makes every reference part of `d` write-live there: with `const e = &data[0];` used after the push, `let pair: [&i64[], i32] = [&data, 1]; const t = &pair; t[0].push(4);` is EN0706 at `&data`, and `grow(let p: [&i64[], i32]) { const t = &p; t[0].push(4); }` makes `p.0` Write, so `grow([&d2, 1])` is EN0706 at `&d2` while a reference into `d2` is still used. A `&string` part's machine form follows its permission, as a `&string` parameter's does (section 7.7).

**Body-less positions (N10).** Interface members, abstract methods, `fn` types, closure parameters and `declare` functions have no body to read, so their contract is declared: Read for every parameter and `this`, SPEC 5.4's default sources, no stores, and a writable result, as a body's result derived from parameters is. Only marks change it, and none of them declares a store. `let` before the member name means it may change the object, and nothing more; `let` before a reference parameter means it may change that argument; `move` consumes `this`; `readonly` before the return type makes the result read-only, as in `let next(): readonly &string | null;` for a reader that moves forward and hands out string parts. A `&string` or `&string | null` result, or a tuple result with a `&string` part, whose default sources include a `&string` or `&string | null` parameter not marked `let` may be a part, so it is read-only, as a body's would be. An `fn` type written with type parameters has its row computed generically, before substitution, as SPEC 4.10 requires: the result of `fn(a: &T) => &T` is writable for every `T`, so with `growVia<T>(f: fn(a: &T) => &T, x: &T, v: T)` writing `*f(x) = v`, `growVia(head, &s, "zz")` is EN0615 at the caller when `head(s: &string): &string` returns `s.slice(0, 1)`, a read-only part. Library declarations carry their marks explicitly (section 10).

**Fitting (N10).** An overridable method with a body has as its contract the join of its body and its marks, with the declared sources of section 6.3, its base body's sources plus `this`, and its base body's stores. An implementation, override or coerced function fits if each parameter's permission is at or below the declared one, a writable declared result is met by a writable result, its stores are within the declared stores, bits included, and `move` matches exactly, and a `&string` whose form then differs is bridged by a `StringForm` adapter (section 2.6); otherwise EN0615, as `` `reset` stores `s` into `lx`, which `fn(let lx: &Lexer, s: &string) => void` can't declare `` for a store. Stores are compared with their bits: each of its stores `(j, m)` needs a declared store `(j, m)` that gives the caller at least as much, one that is `read_set_only` only where its own is, `fields_only` only where its own is `fields_only` or `read_set_only`, and one with the read bit wherever its own has it, since the caller of a `Virtual` or bound call applies the declared store's bits (section 6.2). So an override may store less, its fields where the base keeps the whole parameter, or a plain field where the base stores into a `readonly &` field, but never more: `` `Holder.adopt` keeps `o` itself, but `Base.adopt` keeps only what `o`'s fields hold `` and `` `Sub.retarget` keeps `v` in the `readonly &` field `extra`, but `Base.retarget` keeps it in another field `` are EN0615 at the override (`TESTING.md` `INF-04-fields-only`, `INF-04-read-bit`). This applies wherever stores are compared: overrides, and class bounds `T extends C`, which use the same declared contract. An implementation is every member of the recorded set of satisfactions (section 4), each (class, interface, member), not only a class with an `implements` clause: a class passed to a `&Sizer` parameter, whose `size` writes `this` where `Sizer.size` has no `let`, is EN0615 at the class (`TESTING.md` `FIT-structural`). Since no body-less position declares a store, a function that stores into a borrowing value cannot implement an interface or abstract member or a bound, become an `fn` value or a closure value (an argument, or an arrow kept in a local), or override a base body that stores less; the calls through those positions apply no stores, so this is what keeps them sound. Its result sources must be within the declared set as well, and outside it is EN0714, the code the body check of section 6.3 gives, reported once, with the return as primary span and the declaring member as secondary: `` `Strict.label` returns a reference into `fallback`, but `Labeler.label` returns only into `name` or `this` ``, with `` help: through a `&Labeler`, callers only expect `name` or the object ``. An implementing class therefore writes no `let` or `readonly` marks: `public book(id: i64): &Book` meets the interface's `book(id: i64): &Book;`, and an implementation whose result is read-only where the declared result has no `readonly`, as a string part is, is EN0615, `` `Words.next` returns a read-only reference, but `TokenReader` declares a writable one ``, with the help to write `readonly` before the declared return type. An `fn` value converts from a Read row to a Write row only at the top of a coercion site; under `&`, `T[]`, `T | null`, a field, a generic argument or another `fn` type, rows must be equal. Where the conversion changes a `&string` form, of a parameter, a result or a `&string` part of a tuple result, it needs a static target, a function name or a non-capturing arrow, or a closure parameter as the destination, which gets a `StringFormEnv` adapter; from any other `fn` value it is EN0615 (section 2.6). `T extends A & B` takes the lower declared contract, and a class in a bound contributes, for each method, the declared contract of its nearest declaration at or above that class (section 6.3).

**Roles and compiler-inserted calls (N11).** Parameters of `@entry`, `@panicHandler`, `@interrupt` and `test.each` are declared Read, not inferred, and the `unsafe` default does not apply to them; a body that writes one is EN0615. The destructor has exclusive access at end of life, and running it is not a write of the object's own bytes; a write through one of its reference fields sets the class's flag like a method's (above), and so does one in a constructor, which reaches the fields as soon as it has stored them, or in an arrow nested in any of the class's bodies. `==`, `${}`, `...`, `sort` and `console.log` use the prelude contracts: `Eq`, `Ord`, `Clone` and `Display`'s `this` are Read, `Display`'s formatter is written, and an implementation that writes more fails satisfaction, naming the line (EN0615). A synthesised `fmt` (`DisplayGlue`, section 2.6) has exactly that contract, because it only reads the fields and writes the formatter. `@intrinsic` and `@hostCall` take their effects from a compiler table.

**Local functions (N13).** A local function's contract covers its hidden arguments as well as its parameters, so `inc()` conflicts with a live loan on `count` exactly as `inc(&count)` would, and a loop's hold sees it. Its hidden arguments include those of the local functions it calls (section 4), so a `relay()` that calls `grow()` conflicts with a live loan on `q` as `grow()` does. It cannot move a capture (EN0617), rebind a captured reference (EN0616), return a reference derived from one (EN0714) or become an `fn` value (EN0803); a function whose set of hidden arguments is empty can. As a closure argument (C12) it may store nothing, neither a parameter nor a hidden argument, since a call through a closure value applies no stores: a store whose target or stored value is a hidden argument is EN0616 at the argument, as the same store in an arrow is (section 6.4), so `run(bind)` with `function bind(): void { lx.setSrc(&t); }` is rejected, while a direct `bind()` still applies the store.

**The narrowing check (N2).** With contracts in hand, a dataflow on the MIR CFG checks each reference that typing recorded against a narrowing fact (section 4). If the recorded reference is a writer by N3, a later use typed through that fact, with no new test between, is EN0401 naming the writing line. Nothing is re-typed.

**Witnesses.** For each Write permission and each set flag the checker keeps a witness: the shortest chain of calls to a direct write, with the lexically first step as the tiebreak at each step, so a chain always ends. A witness ends at its module's boundary: a call into another module that makes the argument Write is one terminal step, as `` `f` passes `xs` to `grow` from `lib`, which changes it ``, so the choice of the shortest chain inside a module never depends on another module's chains, and no interface file holds another module's witness. Diagnostics print it to show why a function writes, or why a stored argument is borrowed for writing (`` `grow` pushes through `v` ``), and where a witness ends in another module they append that module's own witness, read from its current interface file or its fresh contracts when they print, so a warm build prints what a cold one does after an edit that keeps every contract but changes a chain (`TESTING.md` `OQ16-witness`); `--emit=summary` prints every contract and flag of a module with its witnesses.

### 6.8 Module interface files

Each user module has one text interface file, which the driver writes after checking the module and reads back while its freshness key matches (section 3's `interface`). It holds:

- in a hashed API section, the contract of everything reachable from the module's `public` items, which among the rest says which results hold only a value's read set (`through_read`) and which stores reach one (their read bits, section 6.3) and which take only one (their `read_set_only` bits), and, for each by-value parameter, whether the body moves or writes it, which G07's help reads, a contract fact like the rest, so the check on load below recomputes these too and the used-items hash leaves them out (below), the flag of each borrowing class and tuple type among them (section 6.7), every declaration that another module's MIR or layouts can depend on, and the private layouts those items need.
